Generalised Complex and Spinor Relations
Thomas C. De Fraja, Vincenzo Emilio Marotta, Richard J. Szabo

Abstract
. Courant algebroid relations are used to define notions of relations between Dirac structures and spinors. It is shown under which circumstances a spinor relation gives a Courant algebroid relation and how it descends to a relation between Dirac structures. A converse to this result is proved: a T-duality relation induces a spinor relation that links the Dirac generating operators defining T-dual Courant algebroids, generalising the isomorphism of twisted cohomology associated with topological T-duality. We introduce the notion of relation between generalised complex structures and characterise their reduction. We also define relations between generalised Kähler structures, and rephrase them in terms of bi-Hermitian structures which induce T-duality relations between 𝒩 = ( 2 , 2 ) {\mathcal{N}}=(2,2) supersymmetric sigma-models. We prove existence results for T-dual structures, and demonstrate compatibility of T-duality relations with Type II supergravity equations.



1.
Introduction

Courant algebroids, as introduced in
[
20
,
40
,
50
,
31
]
, have proved to be an indispensable geometric tool for understanding many aspects of supergravity and string theory, see e.g.
[
16
,
32
]
and references therein, as well as aiding in the understanding of Hamiltonian reduction and its various generalisations, see e.g.
[
19
,
11
,
37
,
48
]
. In this paper we continue our explorations
[
21
,
22
]
of the uses of Courant algebroid relations in providing geometric formulations of T-duality and its generalisations in string theory. To put the content of the present paper into context, let us start by briefly summarising the history of Courant algebroids following
[
35
]
.

1.1.
Courant Algebroids

Let
M
be a smooth manifold. Courant algebroids were first described by Courant and Weinstein in
[
19
,
20
]
in their search for integrability conditions for certain subbundles, called
Dirac structures
, of the bundle
TM\oplus T^{*}M
endowed with a natural skew-symmetric bracket arising from the Lie bracket of vector fields and the Lie derivative of one-forms along vector fields, which was subsequently called the
Courant bracket
.
They were looking for a suitable geometric description of Dirac’s theory of constrained dynamical systems.
These bundles provide a natural framework for unifying Poisson and (pre)symplectic structures.
1
1

1

The graphs of Poisson bivectors and presymplectic two-forms define isotropic subbundles of
TM\oplus T^{*}M
, with respect to the canonical pairing between vectors and one-forms, making them key examples of Dirac structures.
At the same time, Dorfman was independently studying Dirac structures from a more algebraic perspective
[
23
]
, seeking the same integrability condition. The integrability condition they sought is given by closure under the Courant bracket — analogous to the integrability of distributions of the tangent bundle
TM
equipped with the Lie bracket — which describes the integrability of both Poisson and symplectic structures.

After the emergence of Lie algebroids as the infinitesimal counterparts of Lie groupoids, MacKenzie and Xu
[
42
]
defined Lie bialgebroids as the infinitesimal counterparts of Poisson groupoids. Since the double of a Lie bialgebra is a Drinfel’d double
[
24
]
, this naturally led Liu, Weinstein and Xu to consider how this correspondence generalises to the algebroid setting, i.e. they sought to describe the double of a Lie bialgebroid. In
[
40
]
they found this to be a Courant algebroid, and the first axiomatic definition of a Courant algebroid was given therein: A Courant algebroid is a vector bundle
E
over a manifold
M
, equipped with a bracket on its space of sections, a symmetric non-degenerate pairing, and an
anchor map
taking sections of
E
to vector fields on
M
, together satisfying a set of compatibility conditions.

The definition of Courant algebroids subsequently evolved with Roytenberg’s introduction of the non-skew-symmetric Dorfman bracket
[
45
]
, denoted by
\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket
, replacing the skew-symmetric Courant bracket, where skew-symmetry is sacrificed in order to satisfy the Jacobi identity. The Dorfman bracket became the defining bracket for Courant algebroids, and the number of defining axioms was slowly whittled down from five to three
[
54
,
34
]
.

As these developments occurred, other manifestations of Courant algebroids emerged, including a one-to-one correspondence with a certain type of differential graded (dg) symplectic manifolds
[
46
]
.

More relevant to the subject of the present paper, in his letters to Weinstein
[
50
]
, Ševera explains how Courant algebroids appear in the variational problems for two-dimensional non-linear sigma-models. Also relevant to this work, Kosmann-Schwarzberg and Roytenberg
[
33
,
46
]
showed how one can recover the Courant bracket as a “derived bracket” by introducing spinors associated with the pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle structure of Courant algebroids.

Spinors for the generalised tangent bundle

\mathbb{T}M:=TM\oplus T^{*}M

are given by sections of
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M
and the Clifford action of sections of
TM\oplus T^{*}M
is induced by the contraction with the vector field component and the exterior product with the one-form component:

(1.1)

\displaystyle(X+\alpha)\cdot\omega=\iota_{X}\omega+\alpha\wedge\omega\ .

Then the Dorfman bracket on
TM\oplus T^{*}M
is the derived bracket given by two nested commutators of the de Rham differential with this Clifford action:

(1.2)

\displaystyle\llbracket X+\alpha,Y+\beta\rrbracket\cdot\omega\coloneqq[[\mathrm{d}\,,(X+\alpha)\,\cdot\,],(Y+\beta)\,\cdot\,]\,\omega\ ,

for all
X+\alpha,Y+\beta\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(TM\oplus T^{*}M)
and
\omega\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M)
.
This bracket can also be twisted by replacing
\mathrm{d}
with the twisted differential

\mathrm{d}_{H}=\mathrm{d}+H\,\wedge\ ,

where
H
is a closed three-form.

The role of spinors in generalised geometry is two-fold. On the physics side, the spinor fields define the Ramond–Ramond sector of Type II supergravity, and the twisted differential defines the integrability condition for these fields. This, among other structures placed on the Courant algebroid, will allow for the complete geometric description of T-duality for the full bosonic sector of Type II supergravity.
On the mathematical side, they give an equivalent description of generalised complex geometry, as introduced by Gualtieri in his thesis
[
29
]
.

1.2.
Generalised Complex Geometry

Generalised complex geometry concerns the generalisation of complex structures on
TM
to the bundle
TM\oplus T^{*}M
; it allows for the unification of symplectic and complex structures in the framework of Courant algebroids. These are the two structures that are interchanged in mirror symmetry, which has a very similar flavour to the T-duality of string theory. For instance, the Strominger-Yau-Zaslow (SYZ) conjecture
[
53
]
asserts that mirror symmetry has a geometric incarnation as T-duality. This is expanded on in
[
7
]
using the language of generalised complex geometry, or more precisely generalised Kähler geometry
[
30
]
and generalised Calabi-Yau geometry
[
31
]
.
Though we do not discuss mirror symmetry in detail in this paper, generalised complex geometry is an important tool for studying it, and thus a concrete mathematical framework for relating generalised complex structures is an important step.

Generalised Kähler structures can be introduced by mirroring the definition of Kähler structure on the vector bundle
TM\oplus T^{*}M
.
Gualtieri
[
29
]
then defined them as described above and proved the remarkable result that any generalised Kähler structure corresponds to a bi-Hermitian structure.
Generalised complex and Kähler geometries arise naturally in the context of two-dimensional supersymmetric sigma-models. In fact, generalised Kähler structures were discovered (in the language of bi-Hermitian structures) in the pioneering work
[
28
]
characterising the target spaces which support
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
sigma-models.

A non-linear sigma-model in two dimensions with
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
supersymmetry has a pair of
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetries (right-handed and left-handed). It is shown in
[
56
]
that one of the
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry generators can always be written in terms of a generalised complex structure on the (twisted) standard Courant algebroid
\mathbb{T}M
on the target space
M
of the sigma-model. Moreover,
{\mathcal{N}}=2
supersymmetry exists if and only if
M
admits a generalised complex structure
[
56
]
.
It was shown by Gates, Hull and Roček in
[
28
]
that the non-linear sigma-model is invariant under
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
supersymmetry transformations when the target space
M
admits a generalised Kähler structure which is defined by the generalised complex structure associated with one of the supersymmetry generators and the generalised metric appearing in the action functional of the sigma-model.

1.3.
Courant Algebroid Relations

Courant algebroid relations are inspired by the arrows in Weinstein’s symplectic “category”, which are given by Lagrangian correspondences. Symplectic manifolds
(M_{i},\omega_{i})
with symplectic two-forms
\omega_{i}
for
i=1,2
are in
Lagrangian correspondence
if there is a Lagrangian submanifold
L
of the symplectic manifold
(M_{1}\times M_{2},\mathtt{p}_{1}^{*}\,\omega_{1}-\mathtt{p}_{2}^{*}\,\omega_{2})
:
2
2

2

Here we use the canonical projections
\mathtt{p}_{i}\colon M_{1}\times M_{2}\to M_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.

(1.3)

\displaystyle L\ \subset\ M_{1}\times M_{2}\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\mathtt{p}_{1}^{*}\,\omega_{1}-\mathtt{p}_{2}^{*}\,\omega_{2})\big|_{L}=0\ .

For example, the graph
\text{gr}(\varphi)\subset M_{1}\times M_{2}
of a symplectomorphism
\varphi\colon(M_{1},\omega_{1})\to(M_{2},\omega_{2})
always gives a Lagrangian correspondence.

If a Lie group
{\mathsf{G}}
with Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
has a Hamiltonian action on a symplectic manifold
(M,\omega)
with moment map
\mu\colon M\to\mathfrak{g}^{*}
, then
\mu^{-1}(0)
gives a Lagrangian correspondence between
(M,\omega)
and its symplectic reduction
(\mu^{-1}(0)/{\mathsf{G}},\underline{\omega})
. Symplectic reduction can be generalised to any Poisson manifold
P
by considering a Lie group-valued moment map
\mu\colon P\to{\mathsf{G}}^{*}
, where
{\mathsf{G}}
is now a Poisson-Lie group and
{\mathsf{G}}^{*}
integrates the dual space
\mathfrak{g}^{*}

[
41
]
.
This can be generalised further to quasi-Poisson manifolds, as introduced in
[
3
]
, where the Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket of the quasi-Poisson bivector is given by a trivector rather than vanishing as in the Poisson case. When the action of
{\mathsf{G}}
is ‘quasi-Hamiltonian’, the moment map is then a map
\mu\colon P\to{\mathsf{D}}/{\mathsf{G}}
, where
(\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{g})
is a Manin pair
3
3

3

A Manin pair
(\mathfrak{d},\mathfrak{g})
consists of a Lie algebra
\mathfrak{d}
with symmetric pairing of split signature, and a Lagrangian subalgebra
\mathfrak{g}
.
and
{\mathsf{D}}
integrates
\mathfrak{d}

[
2
,
11
]
.

As mentioned in Section
1.1
, the theory of Manin pairs (or equivalently Lie bialgebras) can be encapsulated by the study of pairs
(E,L)
, where
E
is a Courant algebroid equipped with a Dirac structure
L\subset E
.
4
4

4

The usual notion of a Manin pair is obtained by considering Courant algebroids over a point.
Thus an adequate theory of quasi-Poisson reduction should resemble the Lagrangian correspondence of symplectic reduction, replacing the symplectic reduction by Courant algebroid reduction. In
[
37
,
12
]
this reduction was described as a
Courant morphism
, defined as a Dirac structure in
E\times\overline{\underline{E}}
supported on the graph of a function between the underlying manifolds. Here
\underline{E}
is the reduced Courant algebroid and
\overline{\underline{E}}
flips the sign of the pairing on
\underline{E}
, similar to flipping the sign of
\omega_{2}
in the Lagrangian correspondence in (
1.3
).
Courant algebroid relations are the generalisation of Courant morphisms to cases when there is no map between the spaces on which the Courant algebroids are fibred over. Thus the theory of quasi-Hamiltonian reduction is encompassed by Courant algebroid relations.

Ševera argues that T-duality should be an
“almost isomorphism”
between two quasi-Hamiltonian spaces, with the quasi-Hamiltonian spaces being obtained through quasi-Hamiltonian reduction, and the
“almost isomorphism”
described by a Courant algebroid relation
[
49
]
. In
[
21
,
55
]
the description of T-dualities in terms of Courant algebroid relations is completed and extended. In particular, T-duality is described in
[
21
]
as a Courant algebroid relation
R
between
\underline{E}{}_{1}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
, where the Courant algebroids
\underline{E}{}_{1}
and
\underline{E}{}_{2}
come from the reduction of a Courant algebroid
E\to M
. The base manifold
M
is assumed to be foliated by foliations
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
with respect to which the reductions are performed, with
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}=M/{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}=M/{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
. The main result of
[
21
]
is that, given a generalised metric on
\underline{E}{}_{1}
satisfying some invariance conditions, there exists a unique generalised metric on
\underline{E}{}_{2}
such that the Courant algebroid relation
R
behaves like an “isometry” between them. This fully encodes the well-known T-duality prescription for non-linear sigma-models of closed bosonic strings.

1.4.
The Fourier-Mukai Transform

In Section
1.1
we encountered the space of spinors for the exact Courant algebroid
\mathbb{T}M
, namely the module
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M
over the Clifford algebra
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{T}M)
. Consider Type II supergravity with
H
-flux given by a closed three-form
H\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\tt cl}^{3}(M)
. The Ramond–Ramond fluxes are spinors
F\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M)
satisfying the Bianchi identity

(1.4)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}F+H\wedge F=0\ .

As before, the
H
-twisted exterior derivative is
\mathrm{d}_{H}=\mathrm{d}+H\,\wedge\,
,
thus the Ramond–Ramond fluxes are closed under
\mathrm{d}_{H}
, and hence of interest is their (twisted) cohomology class in the cohomology group
{\mathsf{H}}^{\bullet}(M,H)
of the differential complex
({\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M),\mathrm{d}_{H})
.

The Fourier-Mukai transform characterises T-duality as an isomorphism of twisted cohomology of torus bundles. Let
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
be a torus of dimension
k
.
In
[
8
,
14
]
T-duality is formulated for two principal
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
-bundles
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
over a common base manifold
{\mathcal{B}}
. This allows for the introduction of the
correspondence space

M={\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}\ ,

with respective projections
\varpi_{1}
and
\varpi_{2}
to
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
. The correspondence space is a principal
{\mathsf{T}}^{2k}
-bundle over
{\mathcal{B}}
which sits in the commutative diagram

(1.5)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{2}}

Suppose that both
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
are endowed with
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
-invariant closed three-forms
\underline{H}{}_{1}
and
\underline{H}{}_{2},
respectively.
Then
({\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1})
and
({\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2})
are said to be T-dual if

(1.6)

\displaystyle\varpi_{1}^{*}\,\underline{H}{}_{1}-\varpi_{2}^{*}\,\underline{H}{}_{2}=\mathrm{d}B\ ,

for some
{\mathsf{T}}^{2k}
-invariant two-form
B\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{2k}}(M)
whose restriction to
\ker(\varpi_{1*})\otimes\ker(\varpi_{2*})
is non-degenerate.

The Fourier-Mukai transform

\varrho\colon{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})\longrightarrow{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{2})

is then defined by

(1.7)

\displaystyle\varrho(\omega)\coloneqq\int_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}\,{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\,\wedge\varpi_{1}^{*}\,\omega

for any
\omega\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})
, where the fibrewise integration is the pushforward of forms by the projection
\varpi_{2}\colon M\to{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
; this integration is well-defined because the fibres are compact manifolds. It is shown in
[
8
]
that
\varrho
defines a degree-shifting isomorphism between the twisted differential complexes
\big(\mathsf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1}),\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{1}}\big)
and

\big(\mathsf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{2}),\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{2}}\big)
. It describes the transformation of Ramond–Ramond fields in Type II string theory under fibrewise T-duality.

The Fourier-Mukai transform
\varrho
becomes an isomorphism of irreducible Clifford modules once an isomorphism

(1.8)

\displaystyle\mathscr{R}\colon\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1})\longrightarrow\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2})

of
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})
-modules is chosen such that

(1.9)

\displaystyle\varrho(e\cdot\omega)=(-1)^{k}\,\mathscr{R}(e)\cdot\varrho(\omega)\ ,

for any
e\in\mathsf{\Gamma}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1})
and
\omega\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{\bullet}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1}),
where
e\cdot\omega
is the Clifford action defined in (
1.1
). In
[
14
]
this isomorphism was shown to exist and to be unique.

As shown in
[
21
]
, applying the construction of the T-duality relation outlined earlier, where the foliations
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
are generated by the two
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
-actions on
M={\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
, we obtain a Courant algebroid relation
R\colon\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
. In terms of
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
-invariant sections of
\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
, this recovers
\mathscr{R}
. From there we can consider the Clifford algebra
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
generated by
R
. Sections of
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
act on
{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})\times{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{Q}}_{2})
.
5
5

5

Since
R\subset\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times\overline{\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}}
is isotropic, the Clifford algebra
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
is defined by considering
R\subset\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
. The isotropic condition ensures that the Clifford action is well defined.
If
(e_{1},e_{2})\in R
, then by (
1.9
) it follows that

(1.10)

\displaystyle(e_{1},e_{2})\cdot\big(\omega,\varrho(\omega)\big):=\big(e_{1}\cdot\omega,(-1)^{k}\,e_{2}\cdot\varrho(\omega)\big)=\big(e_{1}\cdot\omega,\varrho(e_{1}\cdot\omega)\big)\ \in\ \text{gr}(\varrho)\ .

Thus the graph
\text{gr}(\varrho)\subset{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})\times{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{{\mathsf{T}}^{k}}^{\bullet}({\mathcal{Q}}_{2})
is invariant under this action, and hence defines a spinor representation. One can show that this is an irreducible representation.

This motivates the definition of an
R
-Clifford relation
, for an arbitrary Courant algebroid relation
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
. Here
E_{i}
are Courant algebroids with spinor bundles
{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{i}}
for
\mathsf{Cl}(E_{i})
, for
i=1,2
. An
R
-Clifford relation is then a subbundle
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}\subset{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{1}}\times{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{2}}
providing an irreducible representation for
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
, i.e.
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
is a spinor bundle for
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
.

1.5.
Outline and Summary of Main Results

Let us briefly outline the structure of this paper. In
Section
2
we review the language of Clifford algebras over finite-dimensional vector spaces and introduce the notion of relation between spinors, linking it to the notion of relation between quadratic vector spaces.
Section
3
is dedicated to the basics of Courant algebroids with particular emphasis on the description of Dirac structures, generalised complex structures and generalised Kähler structures. In
Section
4
we turn to the description of Courant algebroid relations and introduce a notion of relation between Dirac structures, generalising existing notions of morphisms of Dirac structures. We then describe in detail the application of Courant algebroid relations to T-duality.

Section
5
tackles the notion of Courant algebroid relation from the spinor standpoint. The notion of Clifford algebra relation is introduced and used to describe related Dirac generating operators. This is then applied to the case of T-duality, in order to extend some of its properties to the setting of
[
21
]
, and supergravity. In
Section
6
Courant algebroid relations for generalised complex structures are introduced and their applications to reduction and T-duality are discussed. Similarly, in
Section
7
the notion of relation between generalised Kähler structures is introduced and its main example in terms of T-duality is given, together with explicit examples.
Appendix
A
discusses some features of fibred integration that are used in the calculations of the main text.

Let us now describe the main results of this paper in more detail, glossing over several technical points which are addressed in the main text.
We start off in Section
2
by introducing a notion of relation between spinors on quadratic vector spaces, i.e. linear Clifford relations.
We then transfer this notion to pseudo-Euclidean vector bundles and find its connection with Courant algebroid relations, which is the main topic of Section
4
. Since Dirac structures are naturally characterised as spinor line bundles, we also find a suitable notion of relation between Dirac structures descending from the Courant algebroid relation between their ambient bundles.

Let
R
be an isotropic subbundle of the product of anchored pseudo-Euclidean vector bundles
E_{1}\to M_{1}
and
E_{2}\to M_{2}
supported on
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
with
\rho(R^{\perp})\subseteq TC
. By taking the intersection of
R
with
E_{1}
times the zero section of
E_{2}
we may define the kernel of
R
and denote it by
{\rm K}_{R}
. We similarly define the cokernel of
R
and denote it by
{\rm CK}_{R}
.

Definition 1.11

(
Definition
4.15
)
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation supported on a submanifold
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Let
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
be Dirac structures for
E_{1}
and
E_{2},
respectively. The restriction of
R
to
L_{i}
for
i=1,2
is given by

(1.12)

\displaystyle R\big\rvert_{L_{i}}\coloneqq\set{(e_{1},e_{2})\in R\ }{\ e_{i}\in L_{i}}\ .

Then
R
is a
relation between the Dirac structures
L_{1}
and
L_{2}
, or a
Dirac relation
, if

(1.13)

\displaystyle R\big\rvert_{L_{1}}=R\cap(L_{1}\times L_{2})\big\rvert_{C}+{\rm CK}_{R}\qquad\text{and}\qquad R\big\rvert_{L_{2}}=R\cap(L_{1}\times L_{2})\big\rvert_{C}+{\rm K}_{R}

are regular vector subbundles supported on
C.
In that case we write
L_{1}\sim_{R}L_{2}
.

We also show that this definition extends the previous notions of “morphism” between Dirac structures, such as morphisms of Manin pairs, and that it fits the description of reduction of Dirac structures.

We then turn to a description of T-duality between Dirac structures as encompassed by

Proposition 1.14

(
Proposition
4.61
)
.

Under the assumptions of
[
21
, Theorem 5.27]
, let
\underline{L}{}_{1}
be an invariant Dirac structure on
\underline{E}{}_{1}
. Then there exists a unique Dirac structure
\underline{L}{}_{2}
on
\underline{E}{}_{2}
such that the T-duality relation
R:\underline{E}{}_{1}\dashrightarrow\underline{E}{}_{2}
is a Dirac relation.

In
Section
5
we turn to the setting of anchored pseudo-Euclidean vector bundles and their spinor relations, in order to build a well-defined notion of Courant algebroid relation from the standpoint of spinors.

Definition 1.15

(
Definition
5.2
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{i}}
be spinor modules for
E_{i}
for
i=1,2
. A vector subbundle
{\sf S}_{R}\subset({\mathsf{S}}_{E_{1}}\times{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{2}})\rvert_{C}
is an
R
-Clifford relation
if it is a spinor module of the typical fibre of
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
, where the Clifford action is defined with the fibres of the bundle
\big(\mathsf{Cl}(E_{1})\times\mathsf{Cl}(E_{2})\big)\big\rvert_{C}
acting on the fibres of
({\mathsf{S}}_{E_{1}}\times{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{2}})\rvert_{C}
. Elements
\nu\in{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{1}}
and
\mu\in{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{2}}
are
R
-related
, denoted
\nu\sim_{R}\mu
, if
(\nu,\mu)\in{\sf S}_{R}
.

This definition fits the reduction of spinors discussed in
[
25
]
.
We connect this definition with Dirac relations via

Proposition 1.16

(
Proposition
5.22
)
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation supported on
C
with an
R
-Clifford relation
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}\subset({\mathsf{S}}_{E_{1}}\times{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{2}})\rvert_{C}
. Let
L_{1}\subset E_{1}
and
L_{2}\subset E_{2}
be Dirac structures, and let
\Omega_{1}\subset{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{1}}
and
\Omega_{2}\subset{\mathsf{S}}_{E_{2}}
be the corresponding pure spinor line bundles. If
\Omega_{1}\sim_{R}\Omega_{2}
, then
L_{1}\sim_{R}L_{2}
.

In order to complete the description of Courant algebroid relations and Dirac relations in terms of spinors, we characterise them in terms of related Dirac generating operators (Definition
5.31
) through

Proposition 1.17

(
Proposition
5.32
)
.

Let
R\subset E_{1}\times\overline{E}_{2}
be an almost Dirac structure supported on
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
with trivial kernel and cokernel. Suppose that
\mathsf{Cl}(R)
has a faithful irreducible spinor representation
{\mathsf{S}}
and that
{\mathsf{S}}
is a
({\mathsf{D}}_{1},{\mathsf{D}}_{2})
-DGO relation, where
{\mathsf{D}}_{i}
is a Dirac generating operator for
E_{i}
. Then
R
is a Courant algebroid relation.

The converse to this result happens to be elusive in this general setting. We thus resort to proving it in the realm of T-duality relations. We first show

Proposition 1.18

(
Proposition
5.43
)
.

In the setting of Section
1.4
, there is a transitive Courant algebroid
R_{\mathcal{B}}
over
{\mathcal{B}}
whose fibre is isomorphic (as a vector bundle) to a fibre of
R
, where

(1.19)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2})}

is a T-duality relation.

This can be taken as motivation for working in the setting of T-duality relations, since the existence of this bundle is not guaranteed in any other setting.
We then show

Theorem 1.20

(
Theorem
5.77
)
.

Let
R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2})
be a T-duality relation, and let
R_{\mathcal{B}}
be the transitive Courant algebroid constructed in Proposition
1.18
; then
R_{\mathcal{B}}
is isomorphic to
T{\mathcal{B}}\oplus{\mathscr{G}}\oplus T^{*}{\mathcal{B}}
where
{\mathscr{G}}
is a bundle of quadratic Lie algebras over
{\mathcal{B}}
(see Section
5.3
). Let
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
be the
R
-Clifford relation defined in (
5.62
), and let
\lambda\in\det({\mathscr{G}}^{\star}_{1})
be such that the spinor line bundle
\Omega^{\lambda}
generated by
\lambda
is invariant. Then
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
is a
(\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{1}},\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{2}})
-DGO relation.

All these elements give the isomorphism of twisted cohomology that is typical of T-duality, with the notable difference that the topology of the fibres of the dual spaces are not constrained to be compact. These results thus not only perfectly fit T-duality for torus bundles, but also Poisson-Lie T-duality where the requirement on
\lambda
translates into the existence of an invariant divergence on one of the quotient spaces.

Motivated by these results, we formulate T-duality for Type II supergravity as

Proposition 1.21

(
Proposition
5.106
)
.

Let
(g_{A},\underline{H}{}_{A},\phi_{A},F_{A})
and
(g_{B},\underline{H}{}_{B},\phi_{B},F_{B})
be Type II bosonic fields on T-dual principal circle bundles
{\mathcal{Q}}_{A}\to{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{Q}}_{B}\to{\mathcal{B}}
over the same base
{\mathcal{B}}
, respectively. Let

(1.22)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{A},\underline{H}{}_{A},V^{+}_{g_{A}},{\rm div}_{\phi_{A}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{B},\underline{H}{}_{B},V^{+}_{g_{B}},{\rm div}_{\phi_{B}})}

be a T-duality relation with
R
-Clifford relation
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
such that
F_{A}\approx_{R}F_{B}
(see Section
5.4
).
Then
(g_{A},\underline{H}{}_{A},\phi_{A},F_{A})
satisfy the Type II supergravity equations (
5.93
)–(
5.94
) for
\nu=-1
if and only if
(g_{B},\underline{H}{}_{B},\phi_{B},F_{B})
satisfy the Type II supergravity equations (
5.93
)–(
5.94
) for
\nu=\mathrm{i}\,
.

Having developed a robust notion of Dirac relation in both usual and spinor terms, we define relations between generalised complex structures in Section
6
.

Definition 1.23

(
Definition
6.1
)
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation supported on
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Let
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}
be generalised complex structures on
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively.
Then
R
is a
generalised complex relation
if
({\mathcal{J}}_{1}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2})(R)=R
.

We describe many of the properties of generalised complex relations, which turn out to be very similar to the properties of generalised isometries shown in
[
21
]
.
This definition perfectly fits in the spinor framework via

Proposition 1.24

(
Proposition
6.16
)
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation, and let
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}
be a generalised complex structure on
E_{i}
with associated pure spinor line bundle
\Omega_{i}
for
i=1,2
. If
\Omega_{i}\sim_{R}\Omega_{2}
, then
R
is a generalised complex relation.

Since T-duality is one of our main frameworks, we may use its tools to prove that a T-duality prescription exists for generalised complex structures as well through

Proposition 1.25

(
Proposition
6.34
)
.

If
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}
is an invariant generalised complex structure on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
, then there is a generalised complex structure
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
such that

(1.26)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2},{\mathcal{J}}_{2})}

is a generalised complex relation.

In Proposition
6.39
we prove that the T-duality relation also keeps track of the type of the generalised complex structures: the type of the T-dual generalised complex structure can be computed from the type of the starting generalised complex structure and data from the decomposition of spinors induced by the existence of a T-duality relation. That is, we fully recover the type change of generalised complex structures in our general T-duality framework.

We naturally complete in Section
7
our discussion by converging on the description of relations for generalised Kähler structures.

Definition 1.27

(
Definition
7.1
)
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation supported on
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-})
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}=({\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-})
be generalised Kähler structures on
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively.
Then
R
is a
generalised Kähler relation
if
R
is a generalised complex relation for both
(E_{1},{\mathcal{J}}^{+}_{1})\dashrightarrow(E_{2},{\mathcal{J}}^{+}_{2})
and
(E_{1},{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-})\dashrightarrow(E_{2},{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-})
.
If
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
are generalised Calabi-Yau structures, then
R
is a
generalised Calabi-Yau relation
.

This definition naturally implies that the generalised Kähler relation is also a generalised isometry between the associated generalised metrics. Moreover, it gives a natural definition for the relation between the associated bi-Hermitian structures.

Again the realm of T-duality is rich with opportunities to test our results, which we do in

Proposition 1.28

(
Proposition
7.15
)
.

If
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
has an invariant generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
, then there is a generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
such that

(1.29)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})}

is a generalised Kähler relation.

This result can be interpreted as encoding T-duality for
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
supersymmetric sigma-models. A similar result holds for generalised Calabi-Yau structures as well (Proposition
7.21
).

Lastly, we discuss in detail some examples of T-duality for generalised Kähler structures in our formulation, such as mirror symmetry (Example
7.22
) and semi-flat Calabi-Yau metrics (Example
7.24
). We also formulate a new example in which, starting from a trivial
{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
-bundle over
{\mathcal{B}}
, we twist it with a closed three-form and endow it with a generalised Calabi-Yau structure. We construct its T-dual generalised Calabi-Yau manifold as a principal circle bundle over
{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{1}
which admits genuine twisted symplectic structures (Example
7.42
).

1.6.
Acknowledgements

The work of T.C.D. was supported by an EPSRC Doctoral Training Partnership Award. The work of V.E.M. was supported by PNRR MUR projects PE0000023-NQSTI and in part by the GACR Grant EXPRO 19-28268X. This article is based upon work from COST Actions CaLISTA CA21109 and THEORY-CHALLENGES CA22113 supported by COST (European Cooperation in Science and Technology).

2.
Linear Algebra of Spinors and Relations

We begin with a discussion on the linear algebra of relations and spinors, and introduce the language of relations for spinors.

2.1.
Linear Relations

Let
(W_{1},\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{1})
and
(W_{2},\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{2})
be finite-dimensional real vector spaces endowed with a symmetric non-degenerate bilinear pairing. Let
W_{1}\times\overline{W}_{2}
be the product vector space
W_{1}\times W_{2}
endowed with the pairing
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{1}-\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{2}
.

Definition 2.1
.

A
linear relation
between
(W_{1},\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{1})
and
(W_{2},\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle_{2})
, denoted
W_{1}\sim_{U}W_{2}
, is a Lagrangian subspace
U\subset W_{1}\times\overline{W}_{2}
. We also write
U:W_{1}\dashrightarrow W_{2}
.

Example 2.2
.

Let
f\colon W_{1}\to W_{2}
be a linear map such that
\langle f(w),f(w^{\prime})\rangle_{2}=\langle w,w^{\prime}\rangle_{1}
for every
w,w^{\prime}\in W_{1}
. Then
\text{gr}(f)\subset W_{1}\times\overline{W}_{2}
defines a Lagrangian subspace, and hence a linear relation.

Example 2.3
.

Let
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
be finite-dimensional vector spaces and let
f\colon V_{1}\to V_{2}
be a linear map. Consider the vector spaces
\mathbb{V}_{i}\coloneqq V_{i}\oplus V^{*}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
, endowed with the pairing

(2.4)

\displaystyle\langle v_{i}+\alpha_{i},w_{i}+\beta_{i}\rangle_{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\iota_{v_{i}}\beta_{i}+\iota_{w_{i}}\alpha_{i})\ ,

for all
v_{i},w_{i}\in V_{i}
and
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i}\in V^{*}_{i}
, where
\iota
denotes the canonical dual pairing (contraction) between vectors and forms. Then
\mathbb{V}_{1}
and
\mathbb{V}_{2}
are related by

(2.5)

\displaystyle U_{f}\coloneqq\set{(v_{1}+\alpha_{1},v_{2}+\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{V}_{1}\times\mathbb{V}_{2}\,\,|\,\,v_{2}=f(v_{1})\ ,\ \alpha_{1}=f^{*}(\alpha_{2})}\ ,

that is a Lagrangian subspace of
\mathbb{V}_{1}\times\overline{\mathbb{V}}_{2}
.

We shall now introduce linear relations between Lagrangian subspaces. To do this, let
U
be a linear relation between
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. We introduce the
kernel
and
cokernel of
U
which are given by

(2.6)

\displaystyle{\rm K}_{U}\coloneqq(W_{1}\times\set{0})\cap U\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\rm CK}_{U}\coloneqq(\set{0}\times W_{2})\cap U\ ,

respectively. We also denote
\ker(U)=\mathtt{p}_{1}({\rm K}_{U})
and
{\rm coker}(U)=\mathtt{p}_{2}({\rm CK}_{U})
, where
\mathtt{p}_{i}\colon W_{1}\times W_{2}\to W_{i}
are the natural projections.

Definition 2.7
.

Let
W_{1}\sim_{U}W_{2}
and let
V_{i}\subset W_{i}
be a Lagrangian subspace, for
i=1,2
. Define

(2.8)

\displaystyle U\rvert_{V_{i}}\coloneqq\set{(w_{1},w_{2})\in U\ \rvert\ w_{i}\in V_{i}}\ ,

for
i=1,2.
Then
U
is a
linear relation
between the Lagrangian subspaces
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
, denoted
V_{1}\sim_{U}V_{2}
, if

(2.9)

\displaystyle U\rvert_{V_{1}}=U\cap(V_{1}\times V_{2})+{\rm CK}_{U}\qquad\text{and}\qquad U\rvert_{V_{2}}=U\cap(V_{1}\times V_{2})+{\rm K}_{U}\ ,

as vector subspaces.

Note that the inclusion
\supseteq
always holds for both equations.

Remark 2.10
.

If
W_{1}\sim_{U}W_{2}
and
V_{1}\sim_{U}V_{2}
for Lagrangian subspaces
V_{1}\subset W_{1}
and
V_{2}\subset W_{2}
, then if
(v_{1},w_{2})\in U\rvert_{V_{1}}
, there is some
v_{2}\in V_{2}
such that
(v_{1},v_{2})\in U
.

If
U=\text{gr}(f)
is the graph of a linear map
f\colon W_{1}\to W_{2}
, then
{\rm CK}_{U}=\set{0}
,
{\rm K}_{U}=\text{gr}(f\rvert_{\ker(f)})
and
\ker(\text{gr}(f))=\ker(f)
. For Lagrangian subspaces
V_{i}\subset W_{i}
, the condition that
U\rvert_{V_{1}}=U\cap(V_{1}\times V_{2})
is equivalent to
f(V_{1})\subseteq V_{2}
. The condition that
U\rvert_{V_{2}}=U\cap(V_{1}\times V_{2})
is equivalent to
f^{-1}(V_{2})\subseteq V_{1}+\ker(f)
. In the case that
f
is an isomorphism these conditions reduce to
f(V_{1})=V_{2}
.

2.2.
Clifford Algebras

Let
V
be a finite-dimensional real vector space. The vector space

\mathbb{V}=V\oplus V^{*}

has a Clifford algebra structure generated by the relation

\texttt{v}\cdot\texttt{v}=\langle\texttt{v},\texttt{v}\rangle\ ,

for
\texttt{v}\in\mathbb{V}
, where
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle
is the natural pairing from Example
2.3
. We denote this Clifford algebra by
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})
.

A
Clifford module
over
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})
is a vector space
{\mathsf{S}}
together with an algebra homomorphism
\Psi\colon\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})\to{\sf End}({\mathsf{S}})
. The elements of
{\mathsf{S}}
are called
spinors
. A Clifford module
{\mathsf{S}}
is called a
spinor module
if it is irreducible, i.e. if there are no non-trivial proper subspaces
{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathsf{S}}
such that
{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}
is also a Clifford module.

Remark 2.11

(
Spinor Lines
)
.

Let
{\rm Lag}(\mathbb{V})
be the set of Lagrangian subspaces of
\mathbb{V}
. For any
L\in{\rm Lag}(\mathbb{V})
, the subspace
\Omega^{L}\subset{\mathsf{S}}
defined by

(2.12)

\displaystyle\Omega^{L}\coloneqq\set{\psi\in{\mathsf{S}}\ }{\ \texttt{v}\cdot\psi=0\ ,\ \forall\,\texttt{v}\in L}

is one-dimensional. For any
\psi\in{\mathsf{S}}
, we define its
null space

N_{\psi}\subset\mathbb{V}
by

(2.13)

\displaystyle N_{\psi}\coloneqq\set{\texttt{v}\in\mathbb{V}\ }{\ \texttt{v}\cdot\psi=0}\ .

Note that
N_{\psi}
is always an isotropic subspace. We call
\psi\in{\mathsf{S}}
a
pure spinor
if
N_{\psi}
is a Lagrangian subspace. If
\psi
is a pure spinor, then so is
c\,\psi
for any
c\in\mathbbm{R}
. Thus each pure spinor has an associated
spinor line

\Omega^{\psi}:=\Omega^{N_{\psi}}\subset{\mathsf{S}}
. Hence there is a one-to-one correspondence between Lagrangian subspaces
L\in{\rm Lag}(\mathbb{V})
and pure spinor lines
\Omega^{L}\subset{\mathsf{S}}
.

Example 2.14
.

The space of forms
{\mathsf{S}}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
defines a spinor module for
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})
, with
\Psi(\texttt{v})({\mathsf{s}})=\texttt{v}\cdot\sf s
, for every
\texttt{v}\in\mathbb{V}
and
{\mathsf{s}}\in{\mathsf{S}}
. Explicitly, the
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})
-module structure is given via the action

(2.15)

\displaystyle(v+\alpha)\cdot\nu=\iota_{v}\nu+\alpha\wedge\nu\ .

More generally, if
W
is any vector space with split signature bilinear form, and
L\subset W
is any Lagrangian subspace of
W
, then
{\mathsf{S}}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}L^{*}
defines a spinor module for
\mathsf{Cl}(W)
, with
\Psi
similarly defined.

Consider
1\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
, the unit spinor. Then

(2.16)

\displaystyle N_{1}=\set{v+\alpha\in\mathbb{V}\ |\ \iota_{v}1+\alpha\wedge 1=0}=V\ ,

and hence
1
is a pure spinor, with associated Lagrangian subspace
V
. The associated line is
\Omega^{V}=\mathbbm{R}
as a subspace of
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
.

At the opposite extreme, let
\lambda\in\det V^{*}
. Then
N_{\lambda}=V^{*}
and
\Omega^{V^{*}}=\det V^{*}
, hence
\lambda
is a pure spinor with associated Lagrangian subspace
V^{*}
.
See
[
29
, Section 2.5]
for a full description of pure spinors associated to Lagrangian subspaces of
\mathbb{V}
.

Remark 2.17

(
Mukai Pairing on Spinors
)
.

There is a bilinear form on spinors, called the Mukai pairing, which is well-behaved under the spin representation. For spinors
{\mathsf{s}},{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{S}}=\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
, it is defined by

(2.18)

\displaystyle(\!({\mathsf{s}},{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime})\!)=\big(\vartheta\,{\mathsf{s}}\wedge{\mathsf{s}}^{\prime}\big)^{\rm top}\ \in\ \det V^{*}\ ,

where
\vartheta
is the antiautomorphism of the Clifford algebra
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})
determined by the map of tensor products
\mathtt{v}_{1}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathtt{v_{k}}\mapsto\mathtt{v}_{k}\otimes\cdots\otimes\mathtt{v_{1}}
, and
(\,\cdot\,)^{\rm top}
indicates taking the top degree component of a form. See
[
29
, Section 2.4]
for further details and properties of this pairing.

2.3.
Linear Clifford Relations

Let
V
and
W
be vector spaces. We can define two distinct
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})\times\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{W})
-module structures on the product
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
through

(2.19)

\displaystyle(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})\cdot(\nu,\mu)=(\texttt{v}\cdot\nu,\pm\,\texttt{w}\cdot\mu)\ ,

for
\texttt{v}\in\mathbb{V}
and
\texttt{w}\in\mathbb{W}
. Then

(2.20)

\displaystyle(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})^{2}\cdot(\nu,\mu)=(\texttt{v}^{2}\cdot\nu,\texttt{w}^{2}\cdot\mu)\ ,

and so this defines a Clifford action if and only if
\texttt{v}^{2}=\langle\texttt{v},\texttt{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{V}}=\langle\texttt{w},\texttt{w}\rangle_{\mathbb{W}}=\texttt{w}^{2}
.

Let
U\subset\mathbb{V}\times\overline{\mathbb{W}}
be an isotropic subspace. Then
\langle\texttt{v},\texttt{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{V}}=\langle\texttt{w},\texttt{w}\rangle_{\mathbb{W}}
for each
u=(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})\in U
. It follows that
U
has a Clifford algebra structure defined by

u^{2}=\langle u,u\rangle:=\langle\texttt{v},\texttt{v}\rangle_{\mathbb{V}}=\langle\texttt{w},\texttt{w}\rangle_{\mathbb{W}}\ .

Denote by
\mathsf{Cl}^{+}(U)
the algebra
\mathsf{Cl}(U)
acting on
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
with
+
parity in Equation (
2.19
), and by
\mathsf{Cl}^{-}(U)
the algebra with opposite parity. In general,
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
is a reducible representation of
\mathsf{Cl}^{+}(U)
and
\mathsf{Cl}^{-}(U)
.

Definition 2.21
.

A subspace
{\sf S}_{U}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
is a
U-Clifford relation of parity
i\in\set{-,+}=\mathbbm{Z}_{2}
if
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is a spinor module for
\mathsf{Cl}^{i}(U)
. Elements
\nu\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
and
\mu\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
are said to be
U
-related
, denoted
\nu\sim_{U}\mu
, if
(\nu,\mu)\in{\sf S}_{U}
.

We show that this definition leads to a notion of
“isomorphism”
of spinor modules through

Proposition 2.22
.

Let
\mathtt{p}_{i}
be the projections from
\mathbb{V}\times\mathbb{W}
to the first and second factor for
i=1,2
respectively, and denote by the same symbols the projections from
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
. Let
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
be a
U
-Clifford relation of parity
j
, with
\mathtt{p}_{i}(U)\neq\set{0}
and
\mathtt{p}_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{U})\neq\set{0}
for
i=1,2
. Then

(2.23)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{U}\cap(\set{0}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*})={\mathsf{S}}_{U}\cap(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\set{0})=\set{(0,0)}\ .

Proof.

Suppose
(0,\mu)\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
for some
\mu\in\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
. By the invariance of
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, we obtain

(2.24)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}\coloneqq\mathsf{Cl}^{j}(U)\cdot(0,\mu)\ \subset\ {\mathsf{S}}_{U}\ .

The set
{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}
is invariant under
\mathsf{Cl}^{j}(U)
, thus by the irreducibility of
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
either
{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}={\mathsf{S}}_{U}
or
{\mathsf{S}}^{\prime}=\set{(0,0)}
. By the assumptions
\mathtt{p}_{2}(U)\neq\set{0}
and
\mathtt{p}_{1}({\mathsf{S}}_{U})\neq\set{0}
, the latter holds, hence
\mu=0
. The other equality follows similarly.
∎

Corollary 2.25
.

If
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is a
U
-Clifford relation with
\mathtt{p}_{i}(U)\neq\set{0}
and
\mathtt{p}_{i}({\mathsf{S}}_{U})\neq\set{0}
for
i=1,2
, and
(\nu,\mu),(\nu,\mu^{\prime})\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, then
\mu=\mu^{\prime}
.

When the kernel
{\rm K}_{U}
and cokernel
{\rm CK}_{U}
of
U
are non-trivial, there is more freedom in choosing
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
to account for their presence. To address this freedom, we can consider only the injective part of the relation. We define
\underline{\rm CK}_{U}=(\set{0}\times\mathbb{W})\cap(U/{\rm K}_{U})
and

(2.26)

\displaystyle U^{\times}=(U/{\rm K}_{U})/\underline{\rm CK}_{U}\ \subset\ \mathbb{V}/{\rm ker}(U)\times\mathbb{W}/{\rm coker}(U)\ .

We denote by
[\texttt{v},\texttt{w}]
the equivalence class of
(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})\in U
, and by
[(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})]
the projection under the quotient map
U\to U^{\times}
. This projection descends to the Clifford algebra, since
\langle u+k,u+k^{\prime}\rangle=\langle u,u\rangle
for any
u\in U
and
k,k^{\prime}
in
{\rm K}_{U}
or
{\rm CK}_{U}
. The quotient is then taken componentwise on the tensor product, i.e.
[u\otimes u^{\prime}]=[u]\otimes[u^{\prime}]
. This therefore yields the Clifford algebra
\mathsf{Cl}(U^{\times})
.

Suppose that there exists an action of
\mathsf{Cl}(U^{\times})
on
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
. We say that this action is
compatible with
\mathsf{Cl}^{i}(U)
if for each
[\texttt{v},\texttt{w}]\in\mathsf{Cl}(U^{\times})
there is an element
(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})\in\mathsf{Cl}^{i}(U)
with
[(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})]=[\texttt{v},\texttt{w}]
such that

(2.27)

\displaystyle(\texttt{v},\texttt{w})\cdot(\nu,\mu)=[\texttt{v},\texttt{w}]\cdot(\nu,\mu)\ .

Definition 2.28
.

A subspace
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
is a
weak
U
-Clifford relation of parity
i
if there exists an action of
\mathsf{Cl}(U^{\times})
on
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
compatible with
\mathsf{Cl}^{i}(U)
for which
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is a spinor module.

Proposition 2.29
.

A
U
-Clifford relation defines a weak
U
-Clifford relation.

Proof.

Suppose that
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is a
U
-Clifford relation in the sense of Definition
2.21
. For
(k,0)\in{\rm K}_{U}
and
(\nu,\mu)\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, we obtain

(2.30)

\displaystyle(k,0)\cdot(\nu,\mu)=(k\cdot\nu,0)\ .

Then
k\cdot\nu=0
by Proposition
2.22
, hence
{\rm K}_{U}
acts trivially on
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, and similarly for
\underline{\rm CK}_{U}
. Hence
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
descends to a spinor module for
\mathsf{Cl}(U^{\times})
.
∎

Example 2.31
.

Suppose
\bm{f}\colon\mathbb{V}\to\mathbb{W}
is an isomorphism given by

\bm{f}=f^{-1}\oplus f^{*}\ ,

where
f\colon W\to V
is an isomorphism. Let
{\mathsf{S}}_{\text{gr}(\bm{f})}
be a
\text{gr}(\bm{f})
-Clifford relation of parity
+
. Then, noting that
\mathsf{Cl}(\text{gr}(\bm{f}))
is the graph of the induced isomorphism from
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{V})
to
\mathsf{Cl}(\mathbb{W})
,

(2.32)

\displaystyle\big(v+\alpha,\bm{f}(v+\alpha)\big)\cdot(\nu,\mu)=\big(\iota_{v}\nu+\alpha\wedge\nu\,,\,\iota_{f^{-1}(v)}\mu+f^{*}(\alpha)\wedge\mu\big)\ ,

for every
(\nu,\mu)\in{\mathsf{S}}_{\text{gr}(\bm{f})}
, and
v+\alpha\in\mathbb{V}
.

If
\nu
is a zero-form then
\mu
is also a zero-form, otherwise there is
w\in W
such that
f(w)\cdot\mu=0
, contradicting Corollary
2.25
. It follows that, on zero-forms,
{\mathsf{S}}_{\text{gr}(\bm{f})}
is generated by
(1,\kappa)
for some
\kappa\in\mathbbm{R}\setminus\set{0}
.

Similarly, if
\nu\in V^{*}
then
\mu\in W^{*}
and
\mu(w)=\kappa\,\nu(f(w))
. Let
w\in W
be such that
\nu(f(w))=1
. Then

(2.33)

\displaystyle(\nu,\mu)=(\nu,f^{*}\nu)\cdot\big(f(w),w\big)\cdot(\nu,\mu)=(\nu,f^{*}\nu)\cdot\big(1,\mu(w)\big)=(\nu,\kappa\,f^{*}\nu)\ .

Thus
\nu\sim_{\text{gr}(\bm{f})}\kappa\,f^{*}\nu
, and hence
\mu=\kappa\,f^{*}\nu
by Corollary
2.25
.

By induction, it follows that
{\mathsf{S}}_{\text{gr}(\bm{f})}=\text{gr}(\kappa\,f^{*})
.

Remark 2.34
.

In light of Example
2.31
, if
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
is a
U
-Clifford relation, then

(2.35)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{\kappa}=\set{(\nu,\kappa\,\nu_{U})\ |\ (\nu,\nu_{U})\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}}

for each
\kappa\in\mathbbm{R}\setminus\set{0}
is also a
U
-Clifford relation of the same parity.
This is similar to the ambiguity addressed in
[
17
, Part II]
regarding the choice of connection on the spinor bundle.

In order to remove this ambiguity, let
n=\dim_{\mathbbm{R}}V
and consider

(2.36)

\displaystyle\det({\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{\kappa})^{*}=\set{(\mu,\tfrac{1}{\kappa^{2^{n}}}\mu_{U})\ |\ (\mu,\mu_{U})\in\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}}\ .

It follows that
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}={\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{\kappa}\otimes|\det({\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{\kappa})^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}
for every
\kappa\in\mathbbm{R}\setminus\set{0}
, where
|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}
indicates the one-dimensional vector space of
\frac{1}{2^{n}}
-densities on
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, i.e. maps
\mu:\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}\to\mathbbm{R}
such that
\mu(\lambda\,{\mathsf{s}})=|\lambda|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\,\mu({\mathsf{s}})
for all
\lambda\in\mathbbm{R}
and
{\mathsf{s}}\in\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
. This motivates

Definition 2.37
.

If
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is a weak
U
-Clifford relation of parity
i
for
U\subset\mathbb{V}\times\overline{\mathbb{W}}
, the
canonical
U
-Clifford relation of parity
i
is

(2.38)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{U}={\mathsf{S}}_{U}\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}

where
n=\dim_{\mathbbm{R}}V
. As a spinor module,
\mathsf{Cl}^{i}(U)
acts trivially on the second component of
{\mathcal{S}}_{U}
.

If
\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
and
\Omega_{\mathbb{W}}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
are pure spinor lines, then
\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}
and
\Omega_{\mathbb{W}}
are
U
-related
if

(2.39)

\displaystyle(\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\ \subset\ {\mathcal{S}}_{U}\ ,

denoted as
\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\sim_{U}\Omega_{\mathbb{W}}
.

Remark 2.40
.

Definition
2.37
does not depend on the choice of
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
. Indeed, suppose there exists some
U
-Clifford relation
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
. Let

(u_{1},u_{2})\otimes(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\ \in\ (\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\ ,

and
\kappa\in\mathbbm{R}\setminus\set{0}
. Then

(2.41)

\displaystyle(u_{1},u_{2})\otimes(\mu_{1},\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\,\mu_{2})=(u_{1},\tfrac{1}{\kappa}\,u_{2})\otimes(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\ \in\ (\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\ .

Hence
(\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det({\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{\kappa})^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\subseteq(\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}
. The opposite inclusion holds similarly, therefore

(2.42)

\displaystyle(\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}=(\Omega_{\mathbb{V}}\times\Omega_{\mathbb{W}})\otimes|\det({\mathsf{S}}_{U}^{\kappa})^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\ .

Proposition 2.43
.

Let
U\colon\mathbb{V}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{W}
be a linear relation. Let
L_{1}\subset\mathbb{V}
and
L_{2}\subset\mathbb{W}
be Lagrangian subspaces, with
\Omega_{1}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}
and
\Omega_{2}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
the corresponding pure spinor lines. Suppose there is a
U
-Clifford relation
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}\subset\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}V^{*}\times\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}W^{*}
.
If
\Omega_{1}\sim_{U}\Omega_{2}
, then
L_{1}\sim_{U}L_{2}
.

Proof.

Take
(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})\in\Omega_{1}\times\Omega_{2}
such that
(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
and consider
(\texttt{l},\texttt{w})\in U|_{L_{1}}
. Then

(2.44)

\displaystyle(\texttt{l},\texttt{w})\cdot(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=(0,\pm\,\texttt{w}\cdot\psi_{2})\ .

Since
(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, by Proposition
2.22
it follows that
\texttt{w}\cdot\psi_{2}=0
and hence
\texttt{w}\in L_{2}
, i.e.
U|_{L_{1}}\subset L_{1}\times L_{2}
. Similarly,
U|_{L_{2}}\subset L_{1}\times L_{2}
, and hence
L_{1}\sim_{U}L_{2}
.
∎

Remark 2.45
.

Suppose that
L_{1}\sim_{U}L_{2}
and that either
\mathtt{p}_{1}(U)\supseteq L_{1}
or
\mathtt{p}_{2}(U)\supseteq L_{2}
; when
U
is a Lagrangian subspace with trivial kernel and cokernel, this holds automatically. Assume without loss of generality the latter holds. Let
\psi_{1}\in\Omega_{1}
.
By Proposition
2.22
, there is some
\psi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}^{\prime})\in{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
. Since
\mathtt{p}_{2}(U)\supseteq L_{2}
, for each
\texttt{l}_{2}\in L_{2}
there is
\texttt{l}_{1}\in L_{1}
such that
(\texttt{l}_{1},\texttt{l}_{2})\in U\cap(L_{1}\times L_{2})
, and hence

(2.46)

\displaystyle(\texttt{l}_{1},\texttt{l}_{2})\cdot(\psi_{1},\psi_{2}^{\prime})=(0,\pm\,\texttt{l}_{2}\cdot\psi_{2}^{\prime})

must vanish. Thus
\psi_{2}^{\prime}\in\Omega_{2}
so that
\Omega_{1}\sim_{U}\Omega_{2}
.

Remark 2.47
.

For a weak
U
-Clifford relation
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
, given
(\texttt{l},\texttt{w})\in U|_{L_{1}}
and
[\texttt{l},\texttt{w}]\in U|_{L_{1}}^{\times}
, in general the equality

(2.48)

\displaystyle[\texttt{l},\texttt{w}]\cdot(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})=(\texttt{l},\texttt{w})\cdot(\psi_{1},\psi_{2})

does not hold.
In this light, when
U\colon\mathbb{V}\dashrightarrow\mathbb{W}
is a linear relation and
\mathbb{V}
(resp.
\mathbb{W}
) is equipped with a Lagrangian subspace
L_{1}
(resp.
L_{2}
), we say that
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is an
L_{1}
-weak
(resp.
L_{2}
-weak
)
U
-Clifford relation
if
{\mathsf{S}}_{U}
is a weak
U
-Clifford relation satisfying the condition (
2.48
) (resp. the corresponding condition with
L_{2}
in place of
L_{1}
). Then the conclusion of Proposition
2.43
holds.

Remark 2.49
.

Thus far we have been working over the field of real numbers
\mathbbm{R}
. Everything discussed up to this point in this section holds equally well over the field of complex numbers
\mathbbm{C}
. For later use, let us introduce some notation in the complex case. If
W
is a real vector space, its complexification
W\otimes_{\mathbbm{R}}\mathbbm{C}
is denoted by
W^{\mathbbm{C}}
. For a subspace
V\subset W^{\mathbbm{C}}
, we denote the complex conjugate of
V
by
\overline{V}
.

3.
Courant Algebroids, Dirac Structures and Generalised Complex Geometry

We follow
[
29
,
4
]
for the brief review of this section, discussing how Courant algebroids and spinor bundles are deeply interconnected, as well as some aspects of generalised complex geometry. This builds on the notions from Section
2.2
.

3.1.
Spinors and Courant Algebroids

Let
M
be a smooth manifold with
{\rm dim}_{\mathbbm{R}}(M)=n
.
Any pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle
E\to M
of rank
2n
with split signature pairing always admits a
{\sf Spin}(n,n)
-structure
[
29
]
.

For instance, consider the generalised tangent bundle

\mathbb{T}M\coloneqq TM\oplus T^{*}M

equipped with the canonical split signature metric
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle
induced by the duality pairing:

(3.1)

\displaystyle\langle X+\alpha,Y+\beta\rangle\coloneqq\tfrac{1}{2}\,(\iota_{X}\beta+\iota_{Y}\alpha)\ ,

for all
X+\alpha,Y+\beta\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\mathbb{T}M)
.
Then for any
X+\xi\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\mathbb{T}M)
and
\omega\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M),
the action

(3.2)

\displaystyle(X+\xi)\cdot\omega\coloneqq\iota_{X}\omega+\xi\wedge\omega

satisfies

(3.3)

\displaystyle(X+\xi)^{2}\cdot\omega=\langle X+\xi,X+\xi\rangle\,\omega\ .

This makes
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M)
into an irreducible Clifford module, so that

(3.4)

\displaystyle\mathsf{Cl}(TM\oplus T^{*}M)\simeq{\sf End}(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M)\ .

Let
H\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}_{\tt cl}^{3}(M)
be a closed three-form. Introduce the
twisted differential

\mathrm{d}_{H}\colon{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M)\to{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M)
given by

(3.5)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{H}\omega\coloneqq\mathrm{d}\omega+H\wedge\omega\ ,

for any
\omega\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M).
We may then define the twisted Dorfman bracket
\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket_{H}
as a derived bracket of
\mathrm{d}_{H}
and the Clifford action (
3.2
) given by

(3.6)

\displaystyle\llbracket e_{1},e_{2}\rrbracket_{H}\cdot\omega\coloneqq[[\mathrm{d}_{H},e_{1}\,\cdot\,],e_{2}\,\cdot\,]\,\omega\ ,

for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\mathbb{T}M)
and
\omega\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M)
. The bracket
[\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,]
on the right-hand side of (
3.6
) is the graded commutator of operators in
{\sf End}(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M)
, i.e. if
A,B\in{\sf End}(\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M)
are of degree
|A|
and
|B|
respectively, and
\omega\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{\bullet}(M)
, then

(3.7)

\displaystyle[A,B]\cdot\omega=A\cdot B\cdot\omega-(-1)^{|A|\,|B|}\,B\cdot A\cdot\omega\ .

Explicitly

(3.8)

\displaystyle\llbracket X+\alpha,Y+\beta\rrbracket_{H}=[X,Y]+\pounds_{X}\beta-\iota_{Y}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha+\iota_{Y}\,\iota_{X}H\ ,

where
\pounds_{X}
denotes the Lie derivative along a vector field
X\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(TM)
. When
H=0
we drop the subscript in the notation for the bracket.

For any pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle
E\to M
with inner product
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle
of split signature, we can always find a Clifford bundle
\mathsf{Cl}(E)\to M
admitting an irreducible Clifford module
{\mathsf{S}}\to M,
with the Clifford action giving an isomorphism
\mathsf{Cl}(E)\simeq{\sf End}({\mathsf{S}}).
The role of the twisted differential
\mathrm{d}_{H}
in the general case is taken by

Definition 3.9
.

A
Dirac generating operator

{\mathsf{D}}
for the pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle
E\to M
is an odd operator on
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}({\mathsf{S}})
satisfying

(1)

[{\mathsf{D}},f]\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(E),
for all
f\in C^{\infty}(M),
i.e.
[{\mathsf{D}},f]
is the operator given by the Clifford action of some section of
E;

(2)

[[{\mathsf{D}},e_{1}],e_{2}]\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(E),
for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(E);
and

(3)

{\mathsf{D}}^{2}\in C^{\infty}(M),
i.e.
{\mathsf{D}}^{2}
acts as a multiplication by a function.

The structures on the generalised tangent bundle
\mathbb{T}M
are replaced by

Definition 3.10
.

A
Courant algebroid
is a quadruple
6
6

6

Throughout this paper we will denote the Courant algebroid
(E,\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket,\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle,\rho)
simply by
E
when there is no ambiguity. If there is more than one Courant algebroid
E
involved in the discussion, we label its operations with a subscript
{}_{E}\,
.

(E,\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket,\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle,\rho)
, where
E
is a vector bundle over a manifold
M

with a fibrewise non-degenerate pairing
\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\text{\Large$\odot$}^{2}E^{*}),
a vector bundle morphism

\rho\colon E\rightarrow TM

called the
anchor
, and an
\mathbbm{R}
-bilinear bracket operation

(3.11)

\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket\colon\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)\times\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)\longrightarrow\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)

called the
Dorfman bracket
, which together satisfy

(i)

\pounds_{\rho(e)}\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle=\langle\llbracket e,e_{1}\rrbracket,e_{2}\rangle+\langle e_{1},\llbracket e,e_{2}\rrbracket\rangle\ ,

(ii)

\langle\llbracket e,e\rrbracket,e_{1}\rangle=\tfrac{1}{2}\,\pounds_{\rho(e_{1})}\langle e,e\rangle\ ,
and

(iii)

\llbracket e,\llbracket e_{1},e_{2}\rrbracket\rrbracket=\llbracket\llbracket e,e_{1}\rrbracket,e_{2}\rrbracket+\llbracket e_{1},\llbracket e,e_{2}\rrbracket\rrbracket\ ,

for all
e,e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)
.

A Courant algebroid is
regular
if its anchor
\rho:E\to TM
has constant rank, and
transitive
if
\rho
is surjective.

Remark 3.12
.

The Jacobi identity
(iii)
together with the pairing preserving condition
(i)
imply that the anchor
\rho
is a bracket homomorphism:

(3.13)

\displaystyle\rho(\llbracket e_{1},e_{2}\rrbracket)=[\rho(e_{1}),\rho(e_{2})]\ ,

for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)
, where the bracket on the right-hand side is the usual Lie bracket of vector fields in
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(TM)
. We refer to
[
36
, Section 2]
for a complete account of all the main properties of Courant algebroids.

The general result connecting Definitions
3.9
and
3.10
can be formulated as

Theorem 3.14

(​​​
[
4
, Corollary 2.3]
)
.

Let
{\mathsf{D}}
be a Dirac generating operator for the pseudo-Euclidean vector bundle
E\to M.
Then there is a canonical Courant algebroid structure on
E
whose anchor and Dorfman bracket are respectively given by

(3.15)

\displaystyle\pounds_{\rho(e)}f\coloneqq[[{\mathsf{D}},f],e]\ ,

for all
e\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(E)
and
f\in C^{\infty}(M),
and

(3.16)

\displaystyle\llbracket e_{1},e_{2}\rrbracket\coloneqq[[{\mathsf{D}},e_{1}],e_{2}]\ ,

for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(E).

A converse to Theorem
3.14
was first formulated in
[
4
]
. Its circumscription to regular Courant algebroids is given by

Theorem 3.17

(​​​
[
17
, Theorem 41]
)
.

Let
E
be a regular Courant algebroid with split signature pairing. Every spinor bundle
{\mathsf{S}}
over
\mathsf{Cl}(E)
locally admits a Dirac generating operator.

Since any vector bundle with split signature pairing admits a spinor bundle, we can thus construct a Dirac generating operator over any regular split signature Courant algebroid. We will make use of this result in Section
5.3.1
.

Remark 3.18
.

For any Courant algebroid
E
over
M
there is a map
\rho^{*}\colon T^{*}M\to E
given by

(3.19)

\displaystyle\langle\rho^{*}(\alpha),e\rangle\coloneqq\iota_{e}\,\rho^{\tt t}(\alpha)\ ,

for all
\alpha\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{1}(M)
and
e\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)
, where
\rho^{\tt t}\colon T^{*}M\to E^{*}
is the transpose of
\rho.
Then the symmetric part of the Dorfman bracket can be expressed as

(3.20)

\displaystyle\llbracket e_{1},e_{2}\rrbracket+\llbracket e_{2},e_{1}\rrbracket=\rho^{*}\,\mathrm{d}\,\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle\ ,

for all
e_{1},e_{2}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(E)
.

The subbundle
\ker(\rho)^{\perp}\subset E
orthogonal to
\ker(\rho)
with respect to the pairing coincides with the image

(3.21)

\displaystyle\ker(\rho)^{\perp}=\mathrm{im}(\rho^{*})\ .

The pairing preserving condition
(i)
of Definition
3.10
implies

(3.22)

\displaystyle\rho\circ\rho^{*}=0\ ,

from which it follows that the kernel of the anchor is a coisotropic subbundle of
E
:

(3.23)

\displaystyle\ker(\rho)^{\perp}\ \subset\ \ker(\rho)\ .

Remark
3.18
motivates

Definition 3.24
.

A Courant algebroid
E
over
M
is
exact
if the chain complex

(3.25)

\displaystyle 0\longrightarrow T^{*}M\xlongrightarrow{\rho^{*}}E\xlongrightarrow{\rho}TM\longrightarrow 0

is a short exact sequence of vector bundles.

By definition, an exact Courant algebroid is transitive and
\ker(\rho)=\ker(\rho)^{\perp}
is a Lagrangian subbundle of
E
.

An isotropic splitting

\sigma\colon TM\longrightarrow E

of the short exact sequence (
3.25
) induces a closed three-form
H_{\sigma}\in\mathsf{\Omega}_{\tt cl}^{3}(M)
given by

(3.26)

\displaystyle H_{\sigma}(X,Y,Z)=\langle\llbracket\sigma(X),\sigma(Y)\rrbracket,\sigma(Z)\rangle

for
X,Y,Z\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(TM)
. Here
H_{\sigma}=H
from the twisted differential
\mathrm{d}_{H}
, hence
\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket=\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket_{H}
from Equation (
3.6
). An isotropic splitting
\sigma
induces an isomorphism
\sigma\oplus\tfrac{1}{2}\,\rho^{*}
between the exact Courant algebroid
E
and the twisted standard Courant algebroid
(\mathbb{T}M,H_{\sigma})
with anchor the canonical projection
\mathtt{p}_{1}:\mathbb{T}M\to TM
.

In a splitting
E\simeq\mathbb{T}M
, automorphisms
\Phi
of exact Courant algebroids are given by diffeomorphisms
\varphi:M\to M
and
B
-field transformations as

\Phi=\big(\varphi_{*}\oplus(\varphi^{-1})^{*}\big)\circ{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\ .

Here
B
is a two-form seen as a map
B\colon TM\to T^{*}M
, and
{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\,\colon\mathbb{T}M\rightarrow\mathbb{T}M
is the vector bundle map given by

(3.27)

\displaystyle{\,\rm e}\,^{B}\,(X+\alpha)=X+B(X)+\alpha\ .

Remark 3.28

(
Ševera’s Classification
[
50
]
)
.

The isomorphism classes of exact Courant algebroids
E\to\mathbb{T}M
determined by choices of splitting of the short exact sequence (
3.25
) are in one-to-one correspondence with cohomology classes in
{\sf H}^{3}(M,\mathbbm{R}).

Recall that a closed three-form
H_{\sigma}
is defined by Equation (
3.26
).
Since the difference between two splittings
\sigma-\sigma^{\prime}
defines a two-form
B\in\mathsf{\Omega}^{2}(M)
via
(\sigma-\sigma^{\prime})(X)=\rho^{*}(\iota_{X}B)
, the three-form
H_{\sigma}
is shifted by
\mathrm{d}B
under a change of splitting. Hence there is a well-defined cohomology class
[H_{\sigma}]\in{\sf H}^{3}(M,\mathbbm{R})
associated to the exact sequence (
3.25
) which completely determines the exact Courant algebroid. This is called the Ševera class of the exact Courant algebroid. A general discussion can be found in
[
26
, Section 2.2]
.

3.1.1.
Reduction of Courant Algebroids

We briefly explain the reduction of Courant algebroids by isotropic subbundles, following
[
57
]
. We begin with the notion of basic sections for an isotropic subbundle
K
of a Courant algebroid
E
. Let
K^{\perp}\supset K
denote the orthogonal bundle of
K
with respect to the pairing on
E
.

Definition 3.29
.

The space of sections of
K^{\perp}
which are
basic with respect to

K
is given by

(3.30)

\displaystyle\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\text{bas}}(K^{\perp})\coloneqq\set{e\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(K^{\perp})\ |\ \llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),e\rrbracket\subset\mathsf{\Gamma}(K)}\ .

If
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\text{bas}}(K^{\perp})
spans
K^{\perp}
pointwise, then
K\subset E
is involutive and
\rho(K)\subset TM
is an integrable distribution.

The reduction of Courant algebroids by a foliation is the content of the Bursztyn-Cavalcanti-Gualtieri-Zambon Theorem, see
[
10
,
57
]
.

Theorem 3.31

(​​​
[
57
, Theorem 3.7]
)
.

Let
E
be a Courant algebroid over
M
, and let
K
be an isotropic subbundle of
E
.
Suppose that
\rho(K^{\perp})=TM
, that
K^{\perp}
is spanned pointwise by basic sections and that the quotient of
M
by the foliation
\mathcal{F}
integrating
\rho(K)
is smooth with surjective submersion
\varpi\colon M\to\mathcal{Q}
. Then there is a Courant algebroid
\underline{E}
over
\mathcal{Q}
fitting into the pullback diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\perp}/K}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces M}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi}

of vector bundles. The reduced Courant algebroid
\underline{E}
is exact if
E
is exact.

Theorem
3.31
yields a pointwise isomorphism

(3.32)

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}_{q,m}\colon\underline{E}{}_{q}\longrightarrow(K^{\perp}/K)_{m}

for any
q\in{\mathcal{Q}}
and
m\in\varpi^{-1}(q)
.
We denote by
\natural\colon K^{\perp}\to\underline{E}
the vector bundle morphism covering
\varpi\colon M\to{\mathcal{Q}}
given by the quotients by
K
and
{\mathcal{F}}
.
We denote the anchor map inherited from
\rho\colon E\to TM
by
\underline{\rho}\colon\underline{E}\to T{\mathcal{Q}}
through the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces K^{\perp}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces TM}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{E}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces T\mathcal{Q}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\natural\,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varpi_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\underline{\rho}}

We shall now characterise splittings of an exact Courant algebroid that descend to the reduced Courant algebroid.

Definition 3.33
.

Let
E
be an exact Courant algebroid over
M
with an isotropic subbundle
K
. A splitting
\sigma\colon TM\to E
is
adapted to
K
if

(a)

the image of
\sigma
is isotropic,

(b)

\sigma(TM)\subset K^{\perp}\ ,
and

(c)

\sigma(X)\in\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{bas}}(K^{\perp})
for any vector field
X
on
M
which is projectable to
{\mathcal{Q}}
.

If
\sigma
is an adapted splitting, then

(3.34)

\displaystyle\sigma(T{\mathcal{F}})=\sigma\big(\rho(K)\big)\ \subseteq\ K\ ,

by
[
57
, Remark 5.2]
.
The condition of having basic sections spanning
K^{\perp}
pointwise for the reduction to be possible also implies the existence of adapted splittings, as shown in
[
57
, Proposition 5.5]
.
In this case a splitting
\sigma:TM\to E
induces an isotropic splitting
\underline{\sigma}:T{\mathcal{Q}}\to\underline{E}
of the reduced Courant algebroid
\underline{E}
over
{\mathcal{Q}}
by
[
57
, Proposition 5.7]
.

For an exact Courant algebroid
E
with a given splitting
\sigma\colon TM\to E
, we will make use of the
\sigma
-adjoint action
[
21
, Definition 5.21]

(3.35)

\displaystyle\mathsf{ad}^{\sigma}:\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)\times\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)\longrightarrow\mathsf{\Gamma}(E)

which is defined by

(3.36)

\displaystyle{\sf ad}^{\sigma}_{e}\,e^{\prime}\coloneqq\llbracket e,e^{\prime}\rrbracket-\rho^{*}\,{\sigma}^{*}\llbracket e,{\sigma}(\rho(e^{\prime}))\rrbracket\ ,

for all
e,\,e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(E).
If
X\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(TM)
, we denote
\mathsf{ad}_{X}^{\sigma}:=\mathsf{ad}_{\sigma(X)}^{\sigma}
.
See
[
22
, Appendix A]
for some properties of the
\sigma
-adjoint action
{\sf ad}^{\sigma}
. A further useful property is

Proposition 3.37
.

If
K\subset E
is an isotropic subbundle such that
K^{\perp}
is spanned pointwise by basic sections, and
\sigma
is a splitting adapted to
K
, then

\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),\mathsf{\Gamma}(K^{\perp})\rrbracket=\mathsf{ad}^{\sigma}_{\mathsf{\Gamma}(K)}\mathsf{\Gamma}(K^{\perp})\ .

Proof.

Let
e\in\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\text{bas}}(K^{\perp})
and
k\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(K)
. Consider

(3.38)

\displaystyle\llbracket k,e\rrbracket-\mathsf{ad}^{\sigma}_{k}\,e=\rho^{*}\,\sigma^{*}\llbracket k,\sigma(\rho(e))\rrbracket\ .

Since

(3.39)

\displaystyle\rho(\llbracket k,\sigma(\rho(e))\rrbracket)=[\rho(k),\rho(e)]=\rho(\llbracket k,e\rrbracket)\ \in\ \rho(K)\ ,

it follows that
\llbracket k,\sigma(\rho(e))\rrbracket\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(K)
. Since
(\sigma\circ\rho)|_{K}=\mathds{1}_{K}
by
[
21
, Lemma 2.34]
, it follows that
\llbracket k,\sigma(\rho(e))\rrbracket=\sigma\,\rho(\llbracket k,\sigma(\rho(e))\rrbracket)
. The result now follows from
\sigma^{*}\circ\sigma=0
, using that basic sections span
K^{\perp}
pointwise.
∎

3.2.
Dirac Structures

We give a brief introduction to Dirac structures.

Definition 3.40
.

Let
(E,\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket,\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle,\rho)
be a Courant algebroid over a smooth manifold
M
. An
almost Dirac structure

L
is a Lagrangian subbundle
L\subset E.
An almost Dirac structure
L
is
integrable
or a
Dirac structure
if it is also involutive with respect to the Dorfman bracket. A
Dirac structure
L
supported on
N
is an involutive Lagrangian subbundle
L\subset E\rvert_{N},
where
N\subset M
is a smooth (embedded) submanifold such that
\rho(L)\subseteq TN.

Remark 3.41
.

If
L
is a Dirac structure, the triple
(L,\rho\rvert_{L},\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket_{L})
defines a Lie algebroid over
M
, since

(3.42)

\displaystyle\llbracket e_{1},e_{2}\rrbracket+\llbracket e_{2},e_{1}\rrbracket=\rho^{*}\,\mathrm{d}\,\langle e_{1},e_{2}\rangle=0\ ,

for any
e_{1},e_{2}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(L),
i.e. the restriction of the Dorfman bracket to
L
is skew-symmetric and satisfies the Jacobi identity. For a Dirac structure
L
supported on a submanifold
N
, the restriction of the anchor
\rho
is defined in order to provide the data for a Lie algebroid
L
over
N.

Example 3.43

(
Foliations
)
.

Let
M
be a smooth manifold endowed with a regular foliation
{\mathcal{F}}.
Then the (untwisted) standard Courant algebroid
\mathbb{T}M
admits a Dirac structure

L=T{\mathcal{F}}\oplus{\rm Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}})

characterised by the involutivity condition

(3.44)

\displaystyle\llbracket X+\alpha,Y+\beta\rrbracket=[X,Y]+\iota_{X}\,\mathrm{d}\beta-\iota_{Y}\,\mathrm{d}\alpha\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(L)\ ,

for all
X+\alpha,Y+\beta\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(L),
where
{\rm Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}})
is the annihilator of
T{\mathcal{F}}
in
T^{*}M
. The bracket (
3.44
) is skew-symmetric and satisfies the Jacobi identity, i.e. it is a Lie bracket. Thus
L
together with the projection
\mathtt{p}_{1}
to
TM
and the restriction of the Dorfman bracket to
L
defines a Lie algebroid.

Example 3.45

(
Pre-Symplectic Structures
)
.

Let
M
be an even-dimensional manifold endowed with a two-form
\omega.
Then the graph

(3.46)

\displaystyle L^{\omega}=\text{gr}(\omega)=\set{X+\iota_{X}\omega\ |\ X\in TM}

is an almost Dirac structure on the standard Courant algebroid
TM\oplus T^{*}M
.
To check involutivity, for all
X,Y\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(TM)
we compute

(3.47)

\displaystyle\llbracket X+\iota_{X}\omega,Y+\iota_{Y}\omega\rrbracket

\displaystyle=[X,Y]+\pounds_{X}\,\iota_{Y}\omega-\iota_{Y}\,\mathrm{d}\,\iota_{X}\omega

(3.48)

\displaystyle=[X,Y]+\iota_{[X,Y]}\omega+\iota_{Y}\,\iota_{X}\,\mathrm{d}\omega\ .

Hence
L^{\omega}
is involutive, i.e. it is a Dirac structure, if and only if
\mathrm{d}\omega=0.
In other words,
L^{\omega}
is a Dirac structure if and only if
(M,\omega)
is a pre-symplectic manifold.

3.2.1.
Dirac Reduction

We discuss reduction of Dirac structures following
[
57
, Proposition 4.1]
.

Proposition 3.49
.

Let
E
be a Courant algebroid over
M
endowed with an isotropic subbundle
K
satisfying the assumptions of Theorem
3.31
. Let
L
be a subbundle of
E
such that
L\cap K^{\perp}
has constant rank. If

(3.50)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),\mathsf{\Gamma}(L\cap K^{\perp})\rrbracket\ \subset\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(L+K)\ ,

then
L
descends to a subbundle

\underline{L}\coloneqq\natural(L\cap K^{\perp}+K)

of the reduced Courant algebroid
\underline{E}
over
{\mathcal{Q}}.

Corollary 3.51
.

If
L
is a Dirac structure for
E
, then
\underline{L}
is a Dirac structure for
\underline{E}
.

Proof.

Consider any
\underline{e},{\underline{e}}^{\prime}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}(\underline{L}).
They can be lifted to
e,e^{\prime}\in\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm bas}(L\cap K^{\perp}),
because
L\cap K^{\perp}
has constant rank. Then

(3.52)

\displaystyle\llbracket e,e^{\prime}\rrbracket_{E}\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm bas}(L\cap K^{\perp})\ ,

since
L
is involutive and

(3.53)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm bas}(K^{\perp}),\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm bas}(K^{\perp})\rrbracket_{E}\ \subseteq\ \mathsf{\Gamma}_{\rm bas}(K^{\perp})\ .

Therefore

(3.54)

\displaystyle\natural(\llbracket e,e^{\prime}\rrbracket_{E})=:\llbracket\underline{e},{\underline{e}}^{\prime}\rrbracket_{\underline{E}}\ \in\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(\underline{L})\ .

Moreover,
\underline{L}
is Lagrangian by construction.
∎

Remark 3.55
.

If
K\subset L\subset K^{\perp},
then Equation (
3.50
) becomes

(3.56)

\displaystyle\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),\mathsf{\Gamma}(L)\rrbracket\ \subset\ \mathsf{\Gamma}(W)\ .

Any subbundle
L
satisfying the above properties is the pointwise span of basic sections
\mathsf{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{bas}}(L)
. That is, there are elements
\ell_{i}\in L
such that
\llbracket\mathsf{\Gamma}(K),\ell_{i}\rrbracket\subset\mathsf{\Gamma}(K)
and
L=\mathrm{Span}_{\mathbbm{R}}\{\ell_{i}\}
pointwise. The proof can be found in
[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
.
7
7

7

In their notation,
S=K
,
D=D^{S}=L
, and canonicity means existence of basic sections. Note that we do not assume that
L
is a Dirac structure, and hence it is not involutive, thus we only have the implication c)
\implies
a) of
[
13
, Theorem 4.1]
and not the full equivalence.

Example 3.57

(
Pre-Symplectic Reduction
)
.

Let us start from Example
3.45
. We also assume that
M
is foliated by
{\mathcal{F}}
such that
{\mathcal{Q}}=M/{\mathcal{F}}
is smooth, with surjective submersion denoted by
\varpi\colon M\to{\mathcal{Q}}.
By setting

K=T{\mathcal{F}}\oplus\set{0}\ ,

the standard Courant algebroid
TM\oplus T^{*}M
reduces to the standard Courant algebroid
T{\mathcal{Q}}\oplus T^{*}{\mathcal{Q}}.
We shall check under which circumstances Proposition
3.49
holds for the Dirac structure
L^{\omega}
, under the additional assumption that
\ker(\omega)=T{\mathcal{F}}
.

In this case

K^{\perp}=TM\oplus\mathrm{Ann}(T{\mathcal{F}})\ ,

hence

(3.58)

\displaystyle L^{\omega}\cap K^{\perp}=\set{X+\iota_{X}\omega\ |\ X\in T{\mathcal{F}}^{\omega}}\ ,

where
T{\mathcal{F}}^{\omega}
is the orthogonal complement of
T{\mathcal{F}}
with respect to the closed two-form
\omega.
Thus

(3.59)

\displaystyle\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbbm{R}}(L^{\omega}\cap K^{\perp})=\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbbm{R}}(T{\mathcal{F}}^{\omega})\ ,

hence
L^{\omega}\cap K^{\perp}
has constant rank.

In order to check that the condition (
3.50
) holds, we compute

(3.60)

\displaystyle\llbracket X_{\mathcal{F}},X+\iota_{X}\omega\rrbracket=[X_{\mathcal{F}},X]+\iota_{[X_{\mathcal{F}},X]}\omega+\iota_{X}\,\pounds_{X_{\mathcal{F}}}\omega

for all
X_{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(T{\mathcal{F}})
and
X\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(T{\mathcal{F}}^{\omega}).
This is an element in
{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(L^{\omega}+K)
if and only if
\pounds_{X_{\mathcal{F}}}\omega=0.
Hence Equation (
3.50
) holds if and only if
\omega
is constant along the leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}.

The reduced subbundle
\underline{L}
is given by

(3.61)

\displaystyle{\underline{L}}=\natural(L^{\omega}\cap K^{\perp}+K)=\set{{\underline{X}}+\iota_{\underline{X}}{\underline{\omega}}\ |\ {\underline{X}}\in T{\mathcal{Q}}}\ ,

since
T{\mathcal{Q}}
and
T{\mathcal{F}}^{\omega}
are pointwise isomorphic, where
\underline{\omega}
is induced by the restriction
\omega\rvert_{T{\mathcal{F}}^{\omega}}=\varpi^{*}{\underline{\omega}}.
Then
\underline{L}
is a Dirac structure on
T{\mathcal{Q}}\oplus T^{*}{\mathcal{Q}},
as shown in Corollary
3.51
.

3.3.
Generalised Complex Structures

We introduce the basic notions of generalised complex geometry, following
[
31
]
and
[
29
, Section 4]
.

Definition 3.62
.

Let
(E,\llbracket\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rrbracket,\langle\,\cdot\,,\,\cdot\,\rangle,\rho)
be an exact Courant algebroid over
M
with
\mathrm{rk}_{\mathbbm{R}}(E)=4n,
where
\mathrm{dim}_{\mathbbm{R}}(M)=2n.
A
generalised almost complex structure
on
E
is an automorphism
{\mathcal{J}}\in\mathsf{Aut}(E)
covering the identity such that
{\mathcal{J}}^{2}=-\mathds{1}
and

(3.63)

\displaystyle\langle{\mathcal{J}}(e),{\mathcal{J}}(e^{\prime})\rangle=\langle e,e^{\prime}\rangle\ ,

for all
e,\,e^{\prime}\in E.
A generalised almost complex structure
{\mathcal{J}}
is
integrable
or a
generalised complex structure
if either of its
\pm\,\mathrm{i}
