7.
Generalised Kähler Relations

In this final section we study how generalised Kähler structures change under Courant algebroid relations, including many examples of their behaviours under geometric T-duality. Among other things, this enables us to exhibit the T-duality of two-dimensional sigma-models with
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
supersymmetry within our framework, as well as mirror symmetry of Calabi-Yau manifolds.

7.1.
Relating Generalised Kähler Structures

Starting from the notion of a Courant algebroid relation between generalised complex structures from Section
6
, it is natural to induce a similar construction for generalised Kähler structures.

Definition 7.1
.

Let
R\colon E_{1}\dashrightarrow E_{2}
be a Courant algebroid relation supported on
C\subseteq M_{1}\times M_{2}
. Let
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-})
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}=({\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-})
be generalised almost Kähler structures on
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
respectively.
Then
R
is a
generalised almost Kähler relation
if it is a generalised almost complex relation for both
R:(E_{1},{\mathcal{J}}^{\pm}_{1})\dashrightarrow(E_{2},{\mathcal{J}}^{\pm}_{2})
.
If
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
are generalised Kähler structures, then
R
is a
generalised Kähler relation
.
If
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
are generalised Calabi-Yau structures, then
R
is a
generalised Calabi-Yau relation
.

It follows from Proposition
6.12
that generalised almost Kähler relations can be equivalently characterised through

Proposition 7.2
.

A generalised almost Kähler relation
R
decomposes pointwise into four isotropic subbundles as

(7.3)

\displaystyle R_{c}^{\mathbbm{C}}=R_{c}^{+\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i}}\oplus R_{c}^{+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}}\oplus R_{c}^{-\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i}}\oplus R_{c}^{-\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}}

at each
c\in C
, where

(7.4)

\displaystyle R_{c}^{+\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i}}=R^{\mathbbm{C}}_{c}\cap\big((L_{1}^{+}\cap L_{1}^{-})\times(L_{2}^{+}\cap L_{2}^{-})\big)_{c}\quad

\displaystyle,\quad R_{c}^{+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}}=R_{c}^{\mathbbm{C}}\cap\big((L_{1}^{+}\cap\overline{L}{}_{1}^{-})\times(L_{2}^{+}\cap\overline{L}_{2}{}^{-})\big)_{c}\ ,

(7.5)

\displaystyle R_{c}^{-\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i}}=R^{\mathbbm{C}}_{c}\cap\big((\overline{L}_{1}{}^{+}\cap L_{1}^{-})\times(\overline{L}{}_{2}^{+}\cap L_{2}^{-})\big)_{c}\quad

\displaystyle,\quad R_{c}^{-\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}}=R_{c}^{\mathbbm{C}}\cap\big((\overline{L}{}_{1}^{+}\cap\overline{L}{}_{1}^{-})\times(\overline{L}{}_{2}^{+}\cap\overline{L}{}_{2}^{-})\big)_{c}\ ,

with
L_{i}^{\pm}
the
+\mathrm{i}
-eigenbundle of
{\mathcal{J}}_{i}^{\pm}
respectively, for
i=1,2
.

The generalized metric
V^{\pm}
of a generalised almost Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}=(L^{+},L^{-})
can be decomposed as

V^{+}{}^{\mathbbm{C}}=(L^{+}\cap L^{-})\oplus(\overline{L}{}^{+}\cap\overline{L}{}^{-})\qquad\text{and}\qquad V^{-}{}^{\mathbbm{C}}=(L^{+}\cap\overline{L}{}^{-})\oplus(\overline{L}{}^{+}\cap L^{-})\ .

From this decomposition and Proposition
7.2
we get

Lemma 7.6
.

Let
R\colon(E_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})\dashrightarrow(E_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
be a generalised almost Kähler relation. Then
R
is a generalised isometry between the generalised metrics
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
defined by
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
respectively.

Proof.

We obtain

(7.7)

\displaystyle R^{\mathbbm{C}}_{c}=\big(R_{c}^{+\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i}}\oplus R_{c}^{-\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}}\big)\,\oplus\,\big(R_{c}^{+\mathrm{i},-\mathrm{i}}\oplus R_{c}^{-\mathrm{i},+\mathrm{i}}\big)=R_{c}^{+}{}^{\mathbbm{C}}\oplus R^{-}_{c}{}^{\mathbbm{C}}=(R^{+}_{c}\oplus R_{c}^{-})^{\mathbbm{C}}\ ,

and hence
R_{c}=R_{c}^{+}\oplus R_{c}^{-}
for all
c\in C
.
∎

Equivalently, let
\tau_{1}=-{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-}\in{\sf Aut}(E_{1})
and
\tau_{2}=-{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{+}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-}\in{\sf Aut}(E_{2})
be the generalised metrics defined by
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
respectively. From the definition of a generalised almost Kähler relation it follows that

(7.8)

\displaystyle(\tau_{1}\times\tau_{2})(R)=({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{+}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-})(R)=({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{+})\,({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-})(R)=R

and hence
R
is a generalised isometry.

Since a generalised almost Kähler relation
R
is necessarily a generalised isometry, by
[
21
, Proposition 4.17]
it follows that both its kernel
{\rm K}_{R}
and cokernel
{\rm CK}_{R}
are trivial.

Remark 7.9

(
Relating Bi-Hermitian Structures
)
.

Since a generalised almost Kähler relation
R\colon(E_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})\dashrightarrow(E_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is a generalised isometry, there is a splitting

(7.10)

\displaystyle R=R^{+}\oplus R^{-}

where
R^{\pm}=R\cap(V_{1}^{\pm}\times V_{2}^{\pm})
for the generalised metrics
V_{1}^{+}
and
V_{2}^{+}
associated to
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
respectively. Since
{\mathcal{J}}^{\pm}_{i}
commute with the metrics
\tau_{i}
, it follows that

({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{\pm})(R^{\pm})=R^{\pm}\ .

Define

T^{\pm}=\rho(R^{\pm})\ \subset\ TC\ ,

so that
\rho\colon R^{\pm}\to T^{\pm}
are isomorphisms covering the identity.

The bi-Hermitian structures
(g_{i},b_{i},J_{i}^{+},J_{i}^{-})
associated with
{\mathcal{K}}_{i}
act on
T^{+}
and
T^{-}
, and since
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{\pm}
preserves
R^{\pm}
respectively, it follows that

(7.11)

\displaystyle\rho\circ({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}\times{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{\pm})\circ(\rho|_{R^{\pm}})^{-1}\colon T^{\pm}\longrightarrow T^{\pm}

is an isomorphism. In other words

(J_{1}^{\pm}\times J_{2}^{\pm})(T^{\pm})=T^{\pm}\ .

In this sense we say that the bi-Hermitian structures are
R
-related.

Because each generalised almost complex structure
{\mathcal{J}}^{\pm}
of a generalised almost Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}=({\mathcal{J}}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}^{-})
induces a grading of the form (
3.75
), there is a bigrading

(7.12)

\displaystyle\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{\bullet}T^{*}M^{\mathbbm{C}}=\bigoplus_{p,q=0}^{n}\,\Omega^{p,q}\ .

From Proposition
6.23
we may then infer

Proposition 7.13
.

There is a bigrading

(7.14)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}_{R}^{\mathbbm{C}}=\bigoplus_{p,q=0}^{n}\,{\mathcal{S}}_{R}^{p,q}

where

{\mathcal{S}}_{R}^{p,q}=\big((\Omega_{1}^{p,q}\times\Omega_{2}^{p,q})\otimes|\det{\mathsf{S}}_{R}^{*}|^{\frac{1}{2^{n}}}\big)\cap{\mathcal{S}}_{R}^{\mathbbm{C}}\ .

7.2.
T-Duality for Generalised Kähler Structures

Discussing T-duality for generalised Kähler structures follows naturally from Proposition
6.34
concerning T-duality for generalised complex structures.

Suppose that
R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2})
is a geometric T-duality relation. By a
D_{1}
-invariant generalised Kähler structure

{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-})
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
we mean a generalised Kähler structure both of whose generalised complex structures
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-}
are
D_{1}
-invariant. Then from Proposition
6.34
we immediately arrive at

Proposition 7.15
.

If
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
has a
D_{1}
-invariant generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
, then there is a generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
such that
R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is a generalised Kähler relation.

This approach to T-duality for generalised Kähler structures fully recovers the approach proposed in
[
38
]
.

Example 7.16

(
T-Dual Bi-Hermitian Structures for Torus Bundles
)
.

In the setting of T-duality for principal torus bundles, there is an explicit formula for the T-dual bi-Hermitian structure given by
[
14
, Example 4.7]

(7.17)

\displaystyle J_{2}^{\pm}=\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}{}^{-1}\,J_{1}^{\pm}\,\big(\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}{}^{-1}\big)^{-1}\ .

where the projections
\underline{\rho}{}^{\pm}_{i}:V_{i}^{\pm}\to T{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}
are isomorphisms from the generalised metrics

(7.18)

\displaystyle V_{i}^{\pm}=\{X_{i}+b_{i}(X_{i})\pm g_{i}(X_{i})\ |\ X_{i}\in T{\mathcal{Q}}_{i}\}\ ,

and
{\mathscr{R}}
is the
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})
-module isomorphism (
5.67
) induced by
R
.

For
(X_{1},X_{2})\in T^{\pm}=\rho(R^{\pm})
, we compute

(7.19)

\displaystyle(J_{1}^{\pm}\times J_{2}^{\pm})(X_{1},X_{2})

\displaystyle=\big(\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}{}^{-1}X_{1}\,,\,\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}{}^{-1}\,J_{1}^{\pm}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}^{-1}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}{}^{-1}X_{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}X_{1}^{\pm}\,,\,\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}{}^{-1}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}{}^{-1}\,\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}^{-1}X_{2}^{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}X_{1}^{\pm}\,,\,\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}\,\mathscr{R}^{-1}\,X_{2}^{\pm}\big)

\displaystyle=\big(\underline{\rho}{}_{1}^{\pm}\times\underline{\rho}{}_{2}^{\pm}\big)\big({\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}X^{\pm}_{1}\,,\,\mathscr{R}\,{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{\pm}X_{1}^{\pm}\big)\ ,

where
X_{i}^{\pm}=\underline{\rho}{}_{i}^{\pm}{}^{-1}X_{i}\in V_{i}^{\pm}
for
i=1,2
.
Since
\mathscr{R}
is induced by the relation
R
, it follows that

(J_{1}^{\pm}\times J_{2}^{\pm})(T^{\pm})=T^{\pm}\ ,

as anticipated by Remark
7.9
.

Remark 7.20

(
T-Duality for Supersymmetric Sigma-Models
)
.

The Lagrangian density of the
\mathcal{N}=(2,2)
supersymmetric sigma-model of
[
28
]
is determined by the Poisson structure

\Pi=[J^{+},J^{-}]\,g^{-1}

associated with the generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}=({\mathcal{J}}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}^{-})

[
39
]
, where
(g,b,J^{+},J^{-})
is the corresponding bi-Hermitian structure. In the setting of Proposition
7.15
, we may think of the generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
as the one determining the Lagrangian density of a supersymmetric sigma-model via
\Pi_{1}=[J_{1}^{+},J_{1}^{-}]\,g_{1}^{-1}
so that its T-dual sigma-model is obtained directly by constructing the T-dual generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
along the lines of Proposition
6.34
and Theorem
5.39
.

The Lagrangian formulation of the
{\mathcal{N}}=(2,2)
supersymmetric sigma-model is tied to the existence of a generalised Kähler potential.
A generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}=({\mathcal{J}}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}^{-})
is associated with the real Poisson structures

\Pi_{\pm}=\tfrac{1}{2}\,(J^{+}\pm J^{-})\,g^{-1}\ .

If these Poisson structures are integrable, then a generalised Kähler potential can be defined from a bisection of the resulting
(1,1)
-morphism of the integrating double symplectic groupoids
[
5
]
. Thus a suitable integration of Courant algebroid relations is needed to describe the “morphism” of generalised Kähler potentials dictated by T-duality, as hinted to in
[
5
]
. This lies outside the scope of the present paper.

Proposition 7.21
.

Let
R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2})
be a geometric T-duality relation. If
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
is a
D_{1}
-invariant generalised Calabi-Yau structure on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
, then there is a generalised Calabi-Yau structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
such that
R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H}{}_{1},{\mathcal{K}}_{1})\dashrightarrow(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},\underline{H}{}_{2},{\mathcal{K}}_{2})
is a generalised Calabi-Yau relation.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
is in particular a generalised Kähler structure, the existence of a generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
is guaranteed by Proposition
7.15
. The integrability of the generalised Kähler structures is determined by the
(\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{1}},\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{2}})
-DGO relation
{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
constructed in Section
5.3
, and since there are nowhere vanishing sections
\psi_{1}^{\pm}\in{\mathsf{\Gamma}}(\Omega_{1}^{\pm})
such that
\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{1}}\psi^{\pm}_{1}=0
, it follows from Proposition
5.3
that
\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}{}_{2}}\psi^{\pm}_{2}=0
for
\psi^{\pm}_{2}
such that
(\psi^{\pm}_{1},\psi^{\pm}_{2})\in{\mathsf{S}}_{R}
. The Mukai pairings are preserved by Proposition
5.97
, and hence
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
is a generalised Calabi-Yau structure.
∎

Example 7.22

(
Mirror Symmetry
)
.

Consider the setting
[
14
]
of a Calabi-Yau manifold
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
with T-duality taken along a special Lagrangian fibration
\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{1}
, i.e.
\dim_{\mathbbm{R}}\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{1}=\frac{1}{2}\dim_{\mathbbm{R}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
, the symplectic form
\omega_{1}
vanishes along
\underline{{\mathcal{F}}}{}_{1}
, and the complex structure
J_{1}
is real. This induces a generalised Calabi-Yau structure with
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{+}={\mathcal{J}}_{\omega_{1}}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{1}^{-}={\mathcal{J}}_{J_{1}}
. From Example
6.59
, the T-dual generalised Calabi-Yau structure has swapped symplectic and complex structures,
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{+}={\mathcal{J}}_{J_{2}}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{2}^{-}={\mathcal{J}}_{\omega_{2}}
. The bigrading of Proposition
7.13
allows us to associate
\Omega_{1}^{p,q}
with
\Omega_{2}^{p,q}
.

For an ordinary Calabi-Yau structure, there is an isomorphism of the ordinary
(p,q)
decomposition with the decomposition into
\Omega^{p,q}
given by

(7.23)

\displaystyle\Omega_{J}^{p-q}\cap\Omega_{\omega}^{n-p-q}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\omega)\cdot\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\omega^{-1})\cdot\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{p,q}T^{*}M^{\mathbbm{C}}\ .

Thus in T-duality we may associate
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{p,q}T^{*}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}^{\mathbbm{C}}
and
\text{\Large$\wedge$}^{n-p,q}T^{*}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}^{\mathbbm{C}}
, giving the usual relation between the Hodge diamonds of Betti numbers in mirror symmetry.

Example 7.24

(
Semi-Flat Calabi-Yau Metrics
)
.

Following
[
26
]
, let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}={\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
where
{\mathcal{B}}\subset\mathbbm{R}^{k}
is the unit
k
-ball. Take local coordinates
\set{x^{1},\ldots,x^{k}}
on
{\mathcal{B}}
and
\set{y^{1},\ldots,y^{k}}
on
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
. For any
c>0
there is a unique solution
u\colon{\mathcal{B}}\to\mathbbm{R}
to the Monge-Ampère equation

(7.25)

\displaystyle\det(u_{ij})=c

with
u|_{\partial{\mathcal{B}}}=0
and
u_{ij}>0
in
{\mathcal{B}}\setminus\partial{\mathcal{B}}
, where
u_{ij}=\frac{\partial^{2}u}{\partial{x^{i}}\,\partial{x^{j}}}
. In the following we set
c=1
without loss of generality.

We can define a
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
-invariant Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
by

(7.26)

\displaystyle g=\sum_{i,j}\,u_{ij}\,\big(\mathrm{d}x^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\mathrm{d}y^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}y^{j}\big)\ .

With the complex structure
J\in{\sf Aut}(T{\mathcal{Q}}_{1})
defined by
J(\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}})=\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}}
and
J(\frac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}})=-\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}
, the metric
g
is Kähler with Kähler form given by

(7.27)

\displaystyle\omega=\sum_{i,j}\,u_{ij}\ \mathrm{d}x^{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{j}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\,\sum_{i,j}\,u_{ij}\ \mathrm{d}z^{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}\overline{z}^{j}\ ,

where
z^{i}=x^{i}+\mathrm{i}\,y^{i}
.

Thus we can define a generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}=({\mathcal{J}}_{J},{\mathcal{J}}_{\omega})
with generalised metric given by
g
. This has Ricci form equal to

(7.28)

\displaystyle{\rm Ric}_{g}=-\mathrm{i}\,\overline{\partial}\,\partial\log\det(u_{ij})=0\ ,

and hence
g
is Ricci flat.
The associated pure spinors are

\psi_{J}=\mathrm{d}z^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}z^{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi_{\omega}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\omega)\ ,

which are both closed and hence
{\mathcal{K}}_{1}
defines a generalised Calabi-Yau structure on
(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},0)
.

Consider
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}={\mathcal{B}}\times\tilde{{\mathsf{T}}}^{k}
, with coordinates
\set{\tilde{y}^{1},\ldots,\tilde{y}^{k}}
on the dual torus
\tilde{{\mathsf{T}}}^{k}
, and define the correspondence space

M={\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}\ .

We take as
B
-field on
M
the two-form

B=\mathrm{d}y^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{1}+\mathrm{d}y^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{2}\ ,

and hence we may write the T-duality relation

(7.29)

\displaystyle R={\rm Span}_{C^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})}\set{(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}})\,,\,(\mathrm{d}x^{i},\mathrm{d}x^{i})\,,\,(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{i}},\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{i})\,,\,(\mathrm{d}y^{i},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}^{i}})}\ .

Since
{\mathcal{J}}_{J}
and
{\mathcal{J}}_{\omega}
are both
{\mathsf{T}}^{k}
-invariant, we can transport them via Proposition
7.15
to a T-dual generalised Kähler structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}
on
(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},0)
. To see what the T-dual structure looks like, we can apply the constructions of Proposition
6.39
. There are two equivalent approaches to this, which illustrate two ways of viewing the T-dual structure.

Firstly, we can write

(7.30)

\displaystyle\psi_{J}=\mathrm{d}z^{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge\mathrm{d}z^{k}=\mu_{\rm mix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\psi_{\omega}=\exp(\mathrm{i}\,\omega)=\exp(\alpha_{\rm mix})

where

(7.31)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm mix}=\mathrm{i}\,\sum_{i,j}\,u_{ij}\ \mathrm{d}x^{i}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{j}=\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge f^{1}+\cdots+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{k}\wedge f^{k}

for
f^{i}=\sum_{j}\,u_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}y^{j}
. Then
\widehat{f}{}^{\,i}=\sum_{j}\,u^{ij}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}^{j}}
, where
(u^{ij})
is the matrix inverse of
(u_{ij})
, and thus
\widetilde{f}{}^{\,i}=\sum_{j}\,u^{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{j}
.
Hence the pure spinors
\tilde{\psi}_{J}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{\omega}
T-dual to
\psi_{J}
and
\psi_{\omega}
are given by

(7.32)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{J}

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{1}+\cdots+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{k}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{k}\big)\ ,

(7.33)

\displaystyle\tilde{\psi}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\Big(\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\mathrm{i}\,\sum_{j}\,u^{1j}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{j}\Big)\wedge\cdots\wedge\Big(\mathrm{d}x^{k}+\mathrm{i}\,\sum_{j}\,u^{kj}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{j}\Big)\ .

Thus
\tilde{\psi}_{J}
and
\tilde{\psi}_{\omega}
correspond to the symplectic structure
\tilde{\omega}
and complex structure
\tilde{J}
respectively given by (in the basis
(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}^{i}})
)

(7.34)

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathds{1}\\
\mathds{1}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tilde{J}=\begin{pmatrix}0&U^{-1}\\
U&0\end{pmatrix}\ ,

where
U_{ij}=u_{ij}
. This defines a Kähler structure with Kähler metric

(7.35)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}=\sum_{i,j}\,u_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}x^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}x^{j}+u^{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{j}

which agrees with the Buscher rules. This again defines a generalised Calabi-Yau structure
{\mathcal{K}}_{2}=({\mathcal{J}}_{\tilde{\omega}},{\mathcal{J}}_{\tilde{J}})
, though it is not immediately clear that
\tilde{g}
is classically Ricci flat. To see this, we may use a different approach whereby we rewrite the two-form
\alpha_{\rm mix}
.

To this end, write

(7.36)

\displaystyle\alpha_{\rm mix}=\mathrm{i}\,b^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}+\cdots+\mathrm{i}\,b^{k}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{k}

where
b^{i}=\sum_{j}\,u_{ji}\,\mathrm{d}x^{j}
. Then the T-dual pure spinors are given by

(7.37)

\displaystyle{\tilde{\psi}}_{J}

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{1}+\cdots+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{k}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{k}\big)\ ,

(7.38)

\displaystyle{\tilde{\psi}}_{\omega}

\displaystyle=\Big(\sum_{j}\,u_{j1}\,\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{1}\big)\wedge\cdots\wedge\Big(\sum_{j}\,u_{jk}\,\mathrm{d}x^{j}+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{k}\Big)\ .

If we define new coordinates
\tilde{x}^{i}=u_{i}\coloneqq\frac{\partial u}{\partial x_{i}}
, so that
\frac{\partial\tilde{x}^{i}}{\partial x^{j}}=u_{ij}
, then

(7.39)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{i}=\sum_{j}\,u_{ij}\,\mathrm{d}x^{j}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad\mathrm{d}x^{i}=\sum_{j}\,u^{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{j}\ ,

hence

(7.40)

\displaystyle{\tilde{\psi}}_{J}=\exp\Big(\mathrm{i}\,\sum_{j}\,u^{ij}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{j}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{i}\Big)\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\tilde{\psi}}_{\omega}=\big(\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{1}+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{1}\big)\wedge\cdots\wedge\big(\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{k}+\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{k}\big)\ .

The associated Kähler metric in these coordinates is therefore

(7.41)

\displaystyle\tilde{g}=\sum_{i,j}\,u^{ij}\,\big(\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}\tilde{x}^{j}+\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{i}\otimes\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{j}\big)\ ,

whose Ricci form is

{\rm Ric}_{\tilde{g}}=-\mathrm{i}\,\overline{\partial}\,\partial\log\det(u^{ij})=0\ ,

so
\tilde{g}
is Ricci flat.

Example 7.42

(
Harmonic Functions and Symplectic Structures
)
.

Consider
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}={\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
for some two-manifold
{\mathcal{B}}
. Take local coordinates
\set{x^{1},x^{2}}
on
{\mathcal{B}}
and
\set{y^{1},y^{2}}
on
{\mathsf{T}}^{2}
. We can endow
{\mathcal{Q}}_{1}
with the complex structures

(7.43)

\displaystyle J^{+}=\begin{pmatrix}0&-\mathds{1}\\
\mathds{1}&0\end{pmatrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad J^{-}=\begin{pmatrix}\eta&0\\
0&\eta\end{pmatrix}

where

\eta=\begin{pmatrix}0&-1\\
1&0\end{pmatrix}\ .

Consider the metric

g=u\,g_{\text{\tiny E}}\ ,

where
g_{\text{\tiny E}}
is the flat Euclidean metric and
u\in C^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}})
.

Then
J^{\pm}
preserve the metric
g
, and we may define the fundamental two-forms
\omega_{\pm}=g\,J^{\pm}
which are given by

(7.44)

\displaystyle\omega_{+}=u\,(\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}+\mathrm{d}y^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{2})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\omega_{-}=u\,(\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}+\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{2})\ .

We may calculate

(7.45)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{-}^{c}\omega_{-}=-\mathrm{d}_{+}^{c}\omega_{+}=(u_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}-u_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2})\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{2}=:\underline{H}\ ,

where
u_{i}=\frac{\partial u}{\partial x^{i}}
.
The three-form
\underline{H}\in{\mathsf{\Omega}}^{3}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})
is closed if and only if

\Delta u=0\ ,

where
\Delta
is the Laplacian on
{\mathcal{B}}
.

It then follows that
{\mathcal{K}}=({\mathcal{J}}^{+},{\mathcal{J}}^{-})
is an
\underline{H}
-twisted generalised Kähler structure, where

(7.46)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{J}}^{\pm}=\frac{1}{2}\,\begin{pmatrix}J^{+}+J^{-}&-(\omega_{+}^{-1}\mp\omega_{-}^{-1})\\
\omega_{+}\mp\omega_{-}&-(J^{+}{}^{\tt t}\pm J^{-}{}^{\tt t})\end{pmatrix}\ .

The corresponding
+\mathrm{i}
-eigenbundles are given by

(7.47)

\displaystyle L^{\pm}={\rm Span}_{C^{\infty}({\mathcal{Q}}_{1})^{\mathbbm{C}}}\{

\displaystyle\mp\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}+\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}}\pm\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}}+\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{2}}\,,\,\mp\,\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}-\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}}\pm\mathrm{i}\,u\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+u\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}\,,

(7.48)

\displaystyle\quad\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}+\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}}+\mathrm{i}\,u\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+u\,\mathrm{d}y^{1}\,,\,\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{2}}\mp\mathrm{i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}}\pm u\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+u\,\mathrm{d}y^{2}\,\}\ ,

which can be written as

L^{\pm}=\text{gr}\big(\exp(\alpha_{\pm})\big|_{E_{\pm}}\big)\oplus\mathrm{Ann}(E_{\pm})\ ,

where

E_{\pm}=\underline{\rho}{}_{1}(L^{\pm})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\alpha_{\pm}=u\,(\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\mathrm{d}y^{1})\wedge(\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\pm\mathrm{d}x^{2})\ .

Hence the generating spinors are given by

(7.49)

\displaystyle\psi^{\pm}=\exp(\alpha_{\pm})\wedge\theta_{\pm}\ ,

where

\theta_{\pm}=\pm\,\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\mathrm{d}x^{2}\pm\mathrm{d}y^{1}-\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}y^{2}

are closed one-forms. We then find

(7.50)

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\underline{H}}\psi^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\pm}\wedge\exp(\alpha_{\pm})\wedge\theta_{\pm}+\underline{H}\wedge\exp(\alpha_{\pm})\wedge\theta_{\pm}

(7.51)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\alpha_{\pm}\wedge\theta_{\pm}+\underline{H}\wedge\theta_{\pm}

(7.52)

\displaystyle=(\pm\,\mathrm{i}\,u_{1}+u_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{2}+(\mp\,\mathrm{i}\,u_{1}-u_{2})\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{2}

(7.53)

\displaystyle=0\ ,

and therefore
{\mathcal{K}}
is a generalised Calabi-Yau structure on
(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H})
.

We now apply T-duality in the
y^{2}
direction. Thus let
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
be a principal circle bundle over
{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{1}
, with coordinate
\tilde{y}^{2}
on the fibres. On the correspondence space

M={\mathcal{Q}}_{1}\times_{{\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{1}}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}

we take the
B
-field

(7.54)

\displaystyle B=\mathrm{d}y^{2}\wedge\varTheta

where

\varTheta=\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{2}-y^{1}\,u_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+y^{1}\,u_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}\ .

Note that
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
therefore has Chern class

(7.55)

\displaystyle[\mathrm{d}\varTheta]=\big[u_{2}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}-u_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}\big]\ \in\ {\mathsf{H}}^{2}({\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{1},\mathbbm{R})\ .

Then
\mathrm{d}B=\underline{H}
, and there is a T-duality relation

(7.56)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces R\colon(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{1},\underline{H})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},0)}

which is generated as a
C^{\infty}({\mathcal{B}}\times{\mathsf{T}}^{1})
-module by

(7.57)

\displaystyle\set{(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial x^{i}}-(-1)^{i}\,y^{1}\,u_{3-i}\,\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}^{2}})\,,\,(\mathrm{d}x^{i},\mathrm{d}x^{i})\,,\,(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{1}})\,,\,(\mathrm{d}y^{1},\mathrm{d}y^{1})\,,\,(\tfrac{\partial}{\partial y^{2}},\varTheta)\,,\,(\mathrm{d}y^{2},\tfrac{\partial}{\partial\tilde{y}^{2}})}_{i=1,2}\ .

By Proposition
6.39
, we may write the T-dual pure spinors as

\tilde{\psi}^{\pm}=\exp(\tilde{\alpha}_{\pm})\ ,

where

(7.58)

\displaystyle\tilde{\alpha}_{\pm}=\mp\,u\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}-\mathrm{i}\,u\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}\pm\mathrm{i}\,u\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}-\mathrm{i}\,(\pm\,\mathrm{i}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}+\mathrm{d}x^{2}\pm\mathrm{d}y^{1})\wedge\varTheta\ .

By T-duality, this defines a generalised Calabi-Yau structure on
(\mathbb{T}{\mathcal{Q}}_{2},0)
, i.e.
\mathrm{d}\tilde{\psi}_{\pm}=0
.
We may decompose
\tilde{\alpha}_{\pm}=\pm\tilde{B}+\mathrm{i}\,\tilde{\omega}_{\pm}
, where

(7.59)

\displaystyle\tilde{B}=y^{1}\,u_{1}\,\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}x^{2}-u\,\mathrm{d}x^{2}\wedge\mathrm{d}y^{1}+\mathrm{d}x^{1}\wedge\mathrm{d}\tilde{y}^{2}\ .

Then
\mathrm{d}\tilde{B}=0
and hence
\mathrm{d}\tilde{\omega}_{\pm}=0
, so that
\tilde{\psi}_{\pm}
define
\pm\tilde{B}
-twists of genuine symplectic structures on
{\mathcal{Q}}_{2}
.

Appendix A
A Note on Fibred Integration

In the main text we use some graded commutativity properties of fibred integration which we summarise here. Let
M
be a fibred manifold with
\dim_{\mathbbm{R}}M=n
and oriented fibres
{\mathcal{F}}
of codimension
n-k
. Denote by
\varpi:M\to M/{\mathcal{F}}
the smooth quotient map.

The fibred integral over
{\mathcal{F}}
of a
p
-form
\omega
on
M
is the
(p{-}k)
-form on
M/{\mathcal{F}}
defined as

(A.1)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,\omega\right)(X_{1},\dots,X_{p-k})=\int_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,\mu_{\omega}\ ,

where
\mu_{\omega}
is a fibre volume form such that

(A.2)

\displaystyle\omega(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k},X^{*}_{1},\dots,X^{*}_{p-k})=\mu_{\omega}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})

for vertical vector fields
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}
, and vector fields
X_{1},\dots,X_{p-k}\in T(M/{\mathcal{F}})
with horizontal lifts
X_{1}^{*},\dots,X_{p-k}^{*}\in TM
, i.e.
\varpi_{*}(X_{i}^{*})=X_{i}
.

Thus if
X\in T(M/{\mathcal{F}})
, then

(A.3)

\displaystyle\left(\iota_{X}\int_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,\omega\right)(X_{2},\dots,X_{p-k})=\left(\int_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,\omega\right)(X,X_{2},\dots,X_{p-k})\ .

On the other hand

(A.4)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,\iota_{X^{*}}\omega\right)(X_{2},\dots,X_{p-k})=\int_{{\mathcal{F}}}\,\mu_{\iota_{X^{*}}\omega}

where

(A.5)

\displaystyle\mu_{\iota_{X^{*}}\omega}(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k})=(\iota_{X^{*}}\omega)(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k},X^{*}_{2},\dots,X^{*}_{p-k})=(-1)^{k}\,\omega(Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k},X^{*},X^{*}_{2},\dots,X^{*}_{p-k})\ .

Thus

(A.6)

\displaystyle\iota_{X}\int_{\mathcal{F}}\,\omega=(-1)^{k}\,\int_{\mathcal{F}}\,\iota_{X^{*}}\omega\ .

Similarly

(A.7)

\displaystyle\xi\wedge\int_{\mathcal{F}}\,\omega=(-1)^{k}\,\int_{\mathcal{F}}\,\varpi^{*}\xi\wedge\omega\qquad\text{and}\qquad\mathrm{d}\int_{\mathcal{F}}\,\omega=(-1)^{k}\,\int_{\mathcal{F}}\,\mathrm{d}\omega

for any form
\xi
on
M/{\mathcal{F}}
.

In particular, this has the consequence of introducing a grading on the smooth Fourier-Mukai transform
\varrho
in (
5.69
).
